Extended surgery theory for simply-connected 4 ​ k 4k -manifolds
Csaba Nagy

Abstract
. Kreck proved that two 2 ​ q 2q -manifolds are stably diffeomorphic if and only if they admit normally bordant normal ( q − 1 ) (q{-}1) -smoothings over the same normal ( q − 1 ) (q{-}1) -type ( B , ξ ) (B,\xi) . We show that stable diffeomorphism can be replaced by diffeomorphism if the normal smoothings have isomorphic Q-forms (which consists of the intersection form of the manifold and the induced homomorphism on H q H_{q} ), when the manifolds are simply-connected, q = 2 ​ k q=2k is even and H q ​ ( B ) H_{q}(B) is free. This proves a special case of Crowley’s Q-form conjecture. The basis of the proof is the construction of an extended surgery obstruction associated to a normal bordism. As an application, we identify the inertia group of a ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) (2k{-}1) -connected 4 ​ k 4k -manifold with the kernel of a certain bordism map. By the calculations of Senger-Zhang and earlier results, these kernels are now known in all cases. For k = 2 , 4 k=2,4 , the combination of these results determines the inertia groups. We also obtain, for a simply-connected 4 ​ k 4k -manifold M M with normal ( q − 1 ) (q{-}1) -type ( B , ξ ) (B,\xi) such that H q ​ ( B ) H_{q}(B) is free, an algebraic description of the stable class of M M , that is, the set of diffeomorphism classes of manifolds stably diffeomorphic to M M . Using this description, we explicitly compute the stable class of manifolds M M with rank- 2 2 hyperbolic intersection form.



1.
Introduction

Diffeomorphism classification of high-dimensional smooth manifolds is one of the basic problems of differential topology. While classical surgery theory provides a way for obtaining a diffeomorphism classification from a homotopy classification, the latter is only known for a few special classes of manifolds, leaving the problem largely open.

In the case of even dimensional manifolds, classification up to a weaker equivalence relation, stable diffeomorphism, has proven to be more attainable. Recall that two closed connected smooth
2q
-manifolds
M
and
N
are stably diffeomorphic if there are integers
k,l\geq 0
such that
M\#k(S^{q}\times S^{q})
is diffeomorphic to
N\#l(S^{q}\times S^{q})
. By Kreck’s theorem
[
20
, Corollary 3]
we have the following:

Theorem

(Kreck
[
20
]
)
.

A pair of
2q
-manifolds are stably diffeomorphic if and only if they have the same normal
(q{-}1)
-type and they admit bordant normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings over it.

(See
[
20
, Section 2]
and Section
5.1
for the definition of normal smoothings etc., and our conventions.)

In this paper we will consider simply-connected
2q
-manifolds, with
q\geq 2
even.
Our main result is that, provided the normal
(q{-}1)
-type has free homology in degree
q
, the addition of a single homological invariant is enough to obtain a classification up to diffeomorphism (h-cobordism, if
q=2
). Namely, we define the
Q-form
associated to a closed, oriented, simply-connected
2q
-manifold
M
, with
q
even, and a map
f:M\rightarrow B
(for example, when
f
is a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing):

E_{q}(M,f)=(H_{q}(M),\lambda_{M},H_{q}(f))

which consists of the intersection form
\lambda_{M}:H_{q}(M)\times H_{q}(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
of
M
together with the induced homomorphism
H_{q}(f):H_{q}(M)\rightarrow H_{q}(B)
(see Section
5.2
). This is an example of an
extended quadratic form
over the abelian group
H_{q}(B)
(see Section
2
). Isomorphism of extended quadratic forms over a fixed abelian group is defined in the obvious way,
and we have the following:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
be simply-connected
2q
-manifolds, with
q\geq 2
even. Suppose that the normal
(q{-}1)
-type of
M_{0}
has free homology in degree
q
. Then
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are diffeomorphic (h-cobordant, if
q=2
) if and only if they have the same normal
(q{-}1)
-type and they admit bordant normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings with isomorphic Q-forms over it.

Theorem
1.1
follows from Theorem
1.2
below, which proves Crowley’s Q-form conjecture (see
[
4
, Problem 11]
) in the special case when
H_{q}(B)
is free. In fact we get a stronger statement, also showing that any normal bordism between the normal smoothings is bordant to an h-cobordism.

Theorem 1.2

(Q-form conjecture for simply-connected
B
and even
q
, with free
H_{q}(B)
)
.

Let
B
be a simply-connected space,
\xi
a stable bundle over it and
q\geq 2
an even integer. Let
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
be two closed oriented
2q
-manifolds. Let
f_{i}:M_{i}\rightarrow B
be a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing over
(B,\xi)
(
i=0,1
), and
F_{0}:W_{0}\rightarrow B
a normal bordism between
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
. Suppose that
H_{q}(B)
is free. If
E_{q}(M_{0},f_{0})\cong E_{q}(M_{1},f_{1})
, then
F_{0}
is normally bordant (rel boundary) to a normal bordism
F^{\prime}:W^{\prime}\rightarrow B
such that
W^{\prime}
is an h-cobordism.

Remark 1.3
.

In the other direction, if there is a normal bordism
F^{\prime}:W^{\prime}\rightarrow B
between
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
such that
W^{\prime}
is an h-cobordism, then the inclusions
M_{i}\rightarrow W^{\prime}
determine a homotopy equivalence
H:M_{0}\rightarrow M_{1}
such that
f_{0}\simeq f_{1}\circ H:M_{0}\rightarrow B
. This shows that the condition
E_{q}(M_{0},f_{0})\cong E_{q}(M_{1},f_{1})
is necessary.

Background

Kreck’s theorem is the basis of a strategy for classifying even-dimensional manifolds up to diffeomorphism, which starts with a stable diffeomorphism classification.
Explicit stable diffeomorphism classification
has been obtained for some classes of manifolds,
for example see Hambleton-Kreck-Teichner
[
15
]
, Kasprowski-Land-Powell-Teichner
[
18
]
for
4
-manifolds with certain fundamental groups.
Then the next step is understanding manifolds in a given stable diffeomorphism class, for which the main tool is Kreck’s modified surgery obstruction (see
[
20
, Theorem 4]
).

In some cases it can be used to show that the diffeomorphism classification is determined by the stable diffeomorphism classification.
By
[
20
, Theorem D]
two simply-connected
2q
-manifolds, with
q
odd, are diffeomorphic if and only if they are stably diffeomorphic and have the same Euler-characteristic. This is not true for arbitrary fundamental group and
q
. The difference between the stable diffeomorphism and diffeomorphism classifications can be measured by the
stable class
of a manifold
M
, consisting of the diffeomorphism classes of manifolds that are stably diffeomorphic to
M
and have the same Euler-characteristic. Examples constructed by Conway-Crowley-Powell-Sixt
[
7
]
,
[
6
]

show that the stable class can be arbitrarily large, and in some cases infinite.

Nevertheless, under some assumptions, the stable diffeomorphism classification still determines the diffeomorphism classification for manifolds that are already sufficiently stabilised. It follows from
[
20
, Corollary 4]
that stably diffeomorphic
2q
-manifolds with finite fundamental group and the same Euler-characteristic are in fact diffeomorphic if they split off two copies of
S^{q}\times S^{q}
, or just one copy, if
q
is even and the manifolds are simply-connected.
These cancellation results have been generalised to other fundamental groups (see eg. Crowley-Sixt
[
10
]
), and also strengthened in the special case of
4
-manifolds
(where diffeomorphism is replaced by s-cobordism or homeomorphism), see eg. Hambleton-Kreck
[
14
]
, Hambleton
[
12
]
.

There are few results where unstable classification has been obtained from the stable classification without assuming that the manifolds are already stabilised. As, in general, the stable and unstable classifications do not coincide, additional invariants are needed for the latter. Wall
[
27
]
, Freedman-Quinn
[
11
]
and Hambleton-Kreck
[
13
]
showed that stably homeomorphic
4
-manifolds with trivial or cyclic fundamental group are classified up to s-cobordism/homeomorphism by their equivariant intersection form. However, by Kreck-Schafer
[
19
]
, this is not true for arbitrary
4
-manifolds.
Our Theorem
1.1
provides a new condition of this type for simply-connected
2q
-manifolds whose normal
(q{-}1)
-type has free homology in degree
q
, for all even
q\geq 2
(which, in the
q=2
case, recovers the earlier results on spin simply-connected
4
-manifolds).

Extended surgery theory

We will prove Theorem
1.2
(the special case of the Q-form conjecture), and hence Theorem
1.1
, by developing a new version of surgery theory. This is the main achievement of the paper, and it will take up Sections
2
-
7
.

We start the proof of Theorem
1.2
by applying surgery below the middle dimension to replace
F_{0}
with a
q
-connected normal bordism
F:W\rightarrow B
. Note that for this
F
Kreck’s modified surgery obstruction is defined
[
20
, Section 6]
, with the property that the obstruction is elementary if and only if
W
is bordant (over
(B,\xi)
, rel boundary) to an h-cobordism. It is defined using the intersection form on
H_{q}(\partial U)
, where
U\subset W
is a collection of embedded copies of
S^{q}\times D^{q+1}
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q}(U)\rightarrow H_{q}(W))
is the surgery kernel
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(H_{q}(F):H_{q}(W)\rightarrow H_{q}(B))
. Then
[
20
, Proposition 8]
relates the obstruction to the intersection form on
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(H_{q}(f_{0}):H_{q}(M_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(B))
. This is the basis of
[
20
, Theorem D]
and other cancellation results.

That is not what we will use though, instead, we will define a new obstruction
[\theta_{W,F}]
(see Section
6
). While the definition of
[\theta_{W,F}]
will follow the overall logic of
[
20
]
, we will make significant changes. The main novelty of
[\theta_{W,F}]
, compared to the modified surgery obstruction and the obstructions in classical surgery theory (see Browder
[
5
]
, Wall
[
29
]
), is that it is no longer defined on the surgery kernel. Quite the opposite, we will use a codimension-
0
submanifold
U\subset W
such that the map
H_{q}(U)\rightarrow H_{q}(W)
(and hence the composition
H_{q}(U)\rightarrow H_{q}(W)\rightarrow H_{q}(B)
) is surjective. Furthermore, the monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
in which
[\theta_{W,F}]
lives is no longer determined by the fundamental group alone, but also depends on
H_{q}(B)
and the Wu class
v_{q}(\xi)
. Its elements are represented by quasi-formations on extended quadratic forms over
H_{q}(B)
(see Sections
2
and
4
).

We will define a group
L_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
, which is the analogue of the classical L-group, and prove that it is the group of invertible elements of
\ell_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
. This will rely on a generalisation of Wall’s
[
29
, Lemma 6.2]
to metabolic forms (see Theorem
3.7
). Moreover, we will show that if
H_{q}(B)
is free, then
L_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
is trivial.

We will also define the subsemigroup of elementary elements of
\ell_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
, and prove that
F
is normally bordant to an h-cobordism if and only if
[\theta_{W,F}]
is elementary. Along with its definition, this step is where the main technical difficulty of working with
[\theta_{W,F}]
lies (see Lemma
6.28
), due to our choice of a more complicated
U
.

Then, similarly to
[
20
, Proposition 8]
, we will relate
[\theta_{W,F}]
to the the Q-form of
f_{0}
. However, since the intersection form of
M_{0}
is nonsingular (in contrast to its restriction to
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits H_{q}(f_{0})
), we will find that
[\theta_{W,F}]
has better algebraic properties. In fact, under some assumptions, we will be able to compute
[\theta_{W,F}]
in terms of the Q-forms of
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
, up to the action of
L_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
(see Theorem
7.6
). In the case when
H_{q}(B)
is free and the Q-forms are isomorphic, this will allow us to deduce that
[\theta_{W,F}]
is elementary and complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
(see Section
8
).

Inertia groups of highly-connected manifolds

As an application, next we show how Theorem
1.2
can be used to reduce the computation of inertia groups of
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifolds, for
q=4,8
, to a bordism calculation. Recall that
\Theta_{n}
denotes the group of homotopy
n
-spheres, and the
inertia group
of an
n
-manifold
M
is the subgroup

I(M)=\{\Sigma\in\Theta_{n}\mid M\#\Sigma\approx M\}\leq\Theta_{n}

Wall
[
26
]
introduced a set of invariants that classify
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifolds, for
q\geq 3
, up to connected sum with homotopy spheres. This reduced the classification of such manifolds to two questions, the realisation of the invariants, and the determination of the inertia groups. We now consider the latter. First we introduce some notation and recall what is known about these inertia groups.

Given an oriented manifold
M
, let
\nu_{M}
denote the stable normal bundle of
M
, and also its classifying map
\nu_{M}:M\rightarrow BSO
. If
M
is
(q{-}1)
-connected and
A=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\pi_{q}(\nu_{M}):\pi_{q}(M)\rightarrow\pi_{q}(BSO))
, then the normal
(q{-}1)
-type of
M
is
(BSO\left<q,A\right>,\xi_{q,A})
(see Definition
9.1
and Proposition
9.2
).

Definition 1.4
.

For a subgroup
A\leq\pi_{q}(BSO)
let
\eta_{2q}^{A}:\Theta_{2q}\rightarrow\Omega_{2q}(BSO\left<q,A\right>;\xi_{q,A})
be the map which sends a homotopy
2q
-sphere
\Sigma
to the bordism class of a normal map
\Sigma\rightarrow BSO\left<q,A\right>
. (This map is a well-defined homomorphism by Propositions
9.2
and
9.5
.)

It follows from a standard obstruction theory argument that if
M
is a
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifold and
A=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\pi_{q}(\nu_{M}))
, then
I(M)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A})
, see Proposition
9.6
. Moreover, Kreck’s
[
20
, Theorem D]
implies that if
q
is odd, then
I(M)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A})
, see Theorem
9.8
a).

The subgroup
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A})
is now known for every
q
and
A
, due to the work of several authors. The final cases were resolved by Senger-Zhang
[
25
]
, see
[
25
]
also for the history and earlier results. In particular,
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A})=0
for every
A
if
q\neq 4,8,9
, hence the inertia groups of
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifolds are determined by the above results in all cases, except when
q=4,8
.

In these cases
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A})
is computed as follows:

Theorem 1.5

(
q=4
: Senger-Zhang
[
25
, Theorem 7.14]
, Crowley-Nagy
[
9
]
,
q=8
: Senger-Zhang
[
25
, Theorem 7.15]
, Crowley-Olbermann)
.

Suppose that
q=4
or
8
and
A\leq\pi_{q}(BSO)\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A})=0
if
A\leq 4\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A})=\Theta_{2q}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if
A\not\leq 4\mathbb{Z}
.

Now the Q-form conjecture can be used to identify
I(M)
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A})
, see Theorem
9.8
b). Namely, given a
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifold
M
with
A=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\pi_{q}(\nu_{M}))
, if
\Sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A})
, then the (unique) normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings of
M
and
M\#\Sigma
over
(BSO\left<q,A\right>,\xi_{q,A})
are normally bordant. If
q
is even, then their Q-forms are defined and isomorphic, and, unless
q\equiv 2
mod
8
, every subgroup of
\pi_{q}(BSO)
is free. So by Theorem
1.2
,
M
is diffeomorphic to
M\#\Sigma
. We get the following:

Theorem 1.6
.

Suppose that
q\geq 3
, and
q\equiv 0
,
4
or
6
mod
8
. Let
M
be a
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifold and
A=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\pi_{q}(\nu_{M}):\pi_{q}(M)\rightarrow\pi_{q}(BSO))
. Then

I(M)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\eta_{2q}^{A}:\Theta_{2q}\rightarrow\Omega_{2q}(BSO\left<q,A\right>;\xi_{q,A})).

So if
q=4,8
, then the combination of Theorems
1.5
and
1.6
determines the inertia groups of
(q{-}1)
-connected
2q
-manifolds. Note that in these cases the condition of Theorem
1.5
on
A=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits\pi_{q}(\nu_{M})
can be rephrased in terms of a Pontryagin class. By Bott-Milnor
[
3
]
, if
M
is a
(4j{-}1)
-connected manifold,
d_{M}
is the divisibility of the Pontryagin class
p_{j}(M)\in H^{4j}(M)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\pi_{4j}(\nu_{M}))=\alpha_{M}\mathbb{Z}\leq\mathbb{Z}\cong\pi_{4j}(BSO)
, then

d_{M}=a_{j}(2j{-}1)!\alpha_{M}
, where
a_{j}=1
if
j
is even and
a_{j}=2
if
j
is odd.
Therefore we have the following:

Theorem 1.7
.

The inertia group of a
3
-connected
8
-manifold (resp.
7
-connected
16
-manifold) is trivial if and only if its Pontryagin class
p_{1}(M)
(resp.
p_{2}(M)
) is divisible by
8
(resp.
24
).

The stable smoothing set and stable class

We can also apply Theorem
1.2
to compute the stable smoothing set and the stable class of a manifold. Fix a
2q
-manifold
M
, let
(B,\xi)
be its normal
(q{-}1)
-type, and let
f:M\rightarrow B
be a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing.

Definition 1.8
.

The
stable class
of
M
, denoted
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M)
, is the set of s-cobordism classes of
2q
-manifolds
N
such that
N
is stably diffeomorphic to
M
and
\chi(N)=\chi(M)
.

The
stable smoothing set
of
M
, denoted
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)
, consists of equivalence classes of normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings
g:N\rightarrow B
such that
g
is normally bordant to
f
and
\chi(N)=\chi(M)
. Two such normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings,
g:N\rightarrow B
and
g^{\prime}:N^{\prime}\rightarrow B
, are equivalent if there is a normal bordism
G:W\rightarrow B
between them such that
W
is an s-cobordism between
N
and
N^{\prime}
.

Note that the normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
f
is unique up to an automorphism of
(B,\xi)
(see Section
5.1
), so if
f^{\prime}:M\rightarrow B
is another normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing, then
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)\cong\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f^{\prime})
.

By Kreck’s theorem a manifold
N
represents an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M)
if and only if there is a normal map
g:N\rightarrow B
that represents an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)
. Hence forgetting the normal map defines a surjective map
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M)
. Since for a fixed
N
the normal map
g:N\rightarrow B
is unique up to an automorphism
(B,\xi)
, the fibres of the forgetful map
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M)
are the orbits of the action of
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)_{[M,f]}
on
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)_{[M,f]}
denotes the stabiliser of the normal bordism class of
f:M\rightarrow B
in
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)
. Therefore
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M)\cong\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)/\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)_{[M,f]}
.

From now on assume that
q
is even and
M
is simply-connected. We will next consider extended quadratic forms over abelian groups, see Section
2
for the definitions. For example, the Q-form
E_{q}(N,g)
of a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
g:N\rightarrow B
is an extended quadratic form over
H_{q}(B)
.

Definition 1.9
.

Let
\underline{E}
be an extended quadratic form over an abelian group
Q
.
We define
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{E})

to be the set of isomorphism classes of extended quadratic forms
\underline{E}{}^{\prime}
over
Q
such that
\underline{E}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}\cong\underline{E}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
for some
k\geq 0
.

If
g:N\rightarrow B
represents an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)
, then there is a normal bordism between
f
and
g
, and the proof of
[
20
, Theorem 2]
shows that it can be turned into an s-cobordism by the compatible subtraction of some tori (and, since
\chi(N)=\chi(M)
, the same number of tori are subtracted at both ends). The subtraction of one torus changes the Q-form of
f
or
g
by adding the hyperbolic form
\underline{H}_{2}
, so by Remark
1.3
we have
E_{q}(N,g)\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}\cong E_{q}(M,f)\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
for some
k\geq 0
, ie.
E_{q}(N,g)
represents an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
. It also follows from Remark
1.3
that the isomorphism class of
E_{q}(N,g)
is independent of the choice of the representative
g:N\rightarrow B
, so taking the Q-form induces a well-defined map
E_{q}:\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
.

The argument from the proof of
[
6
, Theorem 10.2]
shows that this map is surjective. Namely, suppose that
\underline{E}
represents an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
, ie.
\underline{E}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}\cong E_{q}(M,f)\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
for some
k\geq 0
. By performing
k
trivial
(q{-}1)
-surgeries on
f
we get a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing with Q-form
E_{q}(M,f)\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
, and after performing
k
further
q
-surgeries along a basis of a lagrangian in the
\underline{H}_{2k}
component of
\underline{E}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
(which has been identified with
E_{q}(M,f)\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
), we get a
g:N\rightarrow B
representing an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)
, with
E_{q}(N,g)\cong\underline{E}
.

If
g:N\rightarrow B
and
g^{\prime}:N^{\prime}\rightarrow B
represent elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)
, then they are normally bordant. So by Theorem
1.2
, if
H_{q}(B)
is free and
E_{q}(N,g)\cong E_{q}(N^{\prime},g^{\prime})
, then
g
and
g^{\prime}
represent the same element. This means that the map
E_{q}:\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
is also injective. So we get the following:

Theorem 1.10
.

If
H_{q}(B)
is free, then
E_{q}:\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
is a bijection.

If
g:N\rightarrow B
represents an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)
and
h\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)_{[M,f]}
, then
h\circ g
also represents an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)
, hence
E_{q}(N,h\circ g)
represents an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
. So we can define an action of
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)_{[M,f]}
on
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
by composing the homomorphism in an extended quadratic form over
H_{q}(B)
with the induced automorphism of
H_{q}(B)
, and then the map
E_{q}:\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
is
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)_{[M,f]}
-equivariant.
Let
A^{(B,\xi)}_{[M,f]}
denote the image of the map
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)_{[M,f]}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(H_{q}(B))
, then it also acts on
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
, and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))/\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)_{[M,f]}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))/A^{(B,\xi)}_{[M,f]}
. Moreover,
E_{q}
induces a surjective map
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M)\cong\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)/\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(B,\xi)_{[M,f]}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))/A^{(B,\xi)}_{[M,f]}
. It follows from Theorem
1.10
that if
H_{q}(B)
is free, then this map is a bijection:

Theorem 1.11
.

If
H_{q}(B)
is free, then
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M)\cong\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))/A^{(B,\xi)}_{[M,f]}
.

Thus, when
H_{q}(B)
is free, the Q-form conjecture reduces the computation of the stable smoothing set and the stable class to algebraic problems. The former only depends on the Q-form of a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
f:M\rightarrow B
, for the latter we further need to know the subgroup
A^{(B,\xi)}_{[M,f]}\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(H_{q}(B))
and understand its action on
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
.

To illustrate this, in Theorem
10.13
we determine
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
for manifolds whose intersection form is hyperbolic of rank
2
(which includes the manifolds
N_{a,b}
and
M_{a,b}
constructed in Conway-Crowley-Powell-Sixt
[
7
]
). We also show in Proposition
10.14
that in this case any automorphism of
H_{q}(B)
that acts on
\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(E_{q}(M,f))
, in particular any element of
A^{(B,\xi)}_{[M,f]}
, acts trivially. By Theorems
1.10
and
1.11
we get the following:

Theorem 1.12
.

Let
M
be a simply-connected
2q
-manifold, with
q\geq 2
even. Let
(B,\xi)
be its normal
(q{-}1)
-type, and
f:M\rightarrow B
a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing. Suppose that
H_{q}(B)
is free,
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(M)=2
and the induced intersection form on
H_{q}(M)/\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits(H_{q}(M))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is isomorphic to
\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]
. Then

|\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M,f)|=|\mathcal{S}^{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(M)|=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(B)=0$ or $2$}\\
1&\text{if $\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(B)=1$ and $|ab|\leq 1$}\\
2^{r-1}&\text{if $\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(B)=1$ and $|ab|\geq 2$}\end{cases}

where in the
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(B)=1
case we fix an identification
H_{q}(B)\cong\mathbb{Z}
and elements
x,y\in H_{q}(M)
such that
[x],[y]
is a basis of
H_{q}(M)/\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits(H_{q}(M))
in which the induced intersection form is given by the matrix
\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]
, we define
a=f_{*}(x),b=f_{*}(y)\in H_{q}(B)\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and
r
denotes the number of (positive) primes dividing
ab
.

In particular, the stable smoothing set and the stable class of
M
are always finite.

The paper is organised as follows. Sections
2
–
4
are concerned with the algebra underlying our constructions, introducing extended quadratic forms over an abelian group, the group
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M},L)
and the monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, respectively. Section
5
contains preliminaries for the geometric results. In Section
6
we define the extended surgery obstruction associated to a normal bordism, and prove some of its main properties. In Section
7
we give a formula for the surgery obstruction in terms of the Q-forms of
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
. In Section
8
we apply the results of the earlier sections to prove Theorem
1.2
. Finally, Sections
9
and
10
contain the proofs of Theorems
1.6
and
1.12
, respectively.

This paper is based on Chapter 4 of the author’s thesis
[
23
]
. Here we will often omit straightforward arguments, and refer to
[
23
]
for those details. Compared to
[
23
]
, a new Section
3
is added, introducing the groups
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M},L)
, which allow us to prove Theorem
4.24
(
[
23
, Theorem 4.2.21]
) without the assumption that
Q
is free. Sections
4
and
7
are updated accordingly. Section
9
is based on
[
23
, Section 5.2.3]
.

All abelian groups will be assumed to be finitely generated and all manifolds will be assumed to be smooth, compact (closed, unless indicated otherwise) and oriented.

2.
Extended quadratic forms

In this section we define and study (symmetric) extended quadratic forms over an abelian group.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
Q
be an abelian group. An
extended quadratic form over
Q
is a triple

\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)

where
M
is an abelian group,
\lambda:M\times M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a symmetric bilinear function, and
\mu:M\rightarrow Q
is a homomorphism.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\underline{M}
be an extended quadratic form as above.

•

\underline{M}
is called
free
, if the abelian group
M
is free.

•

\underline{M}
is
nonsingular
, if the adjoint of
\lambda
,
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits\lambda:M\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(M,\mathbb{Z})=M^{*}
induces an isomorphism
M/\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits M\cong M^{*}
.

•

A subgroup
V\leq M
is called a
half-rank direct summand
, if it is a direct summand in
M
and
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits V=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits M
.

•

A subgroup
L\leq M
is a
free lagrangian
, if it is a free half-rank direct summand in
M
,
\lambda\big|_{L\times L}=0
and
\mu\big|_{L}=0
.

•

A subgroup
L\leq M
is a
T-lagrangian
, if it is a half-rank direct summand in
M
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits M\leq L
,
\lambda\big|_{L\times L}=0
and
\mu\big|_{L}=0
.

•

\underline{M}
is
metabolic
, if it is nonsingular and there is a T-lagrangian
L\leq M
.

•

\underline{M}
is
hyperbolic
, if it is free, nonsingular and there are two lagrangians
L,L^{\prime}\leq M
such that
M=L\oplus L^{\prime}
(as an internal direct sum).

•

\underline{M}
is
full
, if
\mu
is surjective.

•

\underline{M}
is
even
, if
\lambda(x,x)
is even for every
x\in M
.

•

\underline{M}
is
geometric
with respect to a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, if for every
x\in M
we have
\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(x,x)=v\circ\mu(x)
, where
\varrho_{2}:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is reduction mod
2
.

•

The
rank
of
\underline{M}
, denoted by
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\underline{M}
, is the rank of the group
M
.

If
\underline{M}
is free, then T-lagrangians coincide with free lagrangians (and we simply call them lagrangians).

Remark 2.3
.

Baues
[
2
]
and Ranicki
[
24
]
introduced the notion of an extended quadratic form over a quadratic form parameter
P
.
Using the terminology and notation from Crowley-Nagy
[
8
]
, extended quadratic forms over an abelian group
A
, as defined here, are equivalent to extended quadratic forms over the quadratic form parameter
Q^{+}\oplus A
.
More specifically, the form
(M,\lambda,\mu)
over
A
corresponds to the form
(M,\lambda,(q_{\lambda},\mu))
over
Q^{+}\oplus A
, where
q_{\lambda}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is defined by
q_{\lambda}(x)=\lambda(x,x)
.

Given a symmetric quadratic form parameter
P
, there is a canonical “extended symmetrisation” morphism of quadratic form parameters from
P
to
Q^{+}\oplus SP
. It induces a map that sends forms over
P
to forms over
Q^{+}\oplus SP
. It follows from our results in
[
8
]
that this map is a bijection between forms over
P
and those forms over
Q^{+}\oplus SP
that correspond to geometric (with respect to
v_{P}:SP\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
) forms over the abelian group
SP
.

2.1.
Preliminaries

In this section we will collect a few lemmas which are well-known for ordinary “symmetric forms” (ie. pairs
(M,\lambda)
, where
M
is an abelian group and
\lambda:M\times M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a symmetric bilinear function), see eg.
[
22
]
, and can be directly applied in the extended setting. Before those, we recall the following elementary lemma, which we will often use to check that a subgroup of an abelian group is a direct summand.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
A
be an abelian group and
B\leq A
a subgroup. The following are equivalent:

(1)

B
is a direct summand in
A
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits A\leq B
.

(2)

A/B
is free.

(3)

For every
a\in A
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
if
ka\in B
then
a\in B
. ∎

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be a nonsingular form, and
X\subseteq M
a subset. Then
X^{\perp}
denotes the subgroup
\{y\in M\mid\lambda(x,y)=0\text{ for all $x\in X$}\}
.

For any subset
X
, the subgroup
X^{\perp}
contains
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits M
and it is a direct summand in
M
.
Moreover, if
X
is a subgroup of
M
, then
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits X+\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits X^{\perp}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits M
. Therefore, if
X
is a subset of
M
such that
\mu\big|_{X}=0
, then
X
is a T-lagrangian if and only if
X=X^{\perp}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be a free nonsingular form. If
X\leq M
is a subgroup such that
\lambda\big|_{X\times X}
is nonsingular, then
M=X\oplus X^{\perp}
(as an internal direct sum).
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be a free nonsingular form. Let
X,Y\leq M
be subgroups such that
M=X\oplus Y
(as an internal direct sum). Then

a)
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits\lambda
defines an isomorphism
Y^{\perp}\cong X^{*}
.

b)
M=X^{\perp}\oplus Y^{\perp}
(as an internal direct sum).

c) And if
X\leq X^{\perp}
(hence
X\oplus Y^{\perp}
is a subgroup of
M
by part b)), then
\lambda\big|_{(X\oplus Y^{\perp})\times(X\oplus Y^{\perp})}
is nonsingular.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be a metabolic form. If
L\leq M
is a T-lagrangian and
N\leq M
is a direct complement of
L
, then
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits\lambda
defines an isomorphism
N\cong L^{*}
.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be a free metabolic form of rank
2k
, and let
L\leq M
be a lagrangian. Then
M
has a basis
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k},f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{k}
such that
L=\left<e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k}\right>
, and in this basis
\lambda
is given by the block matrix

\begin{bmatrix}0&I_{k}\\
I_{k}&D\end{bmatrix}

where
I_{k}
is the
k\times k
identity matrix and
D
is a diagonal matrix such that each of its entries is either
0
or
1
.
∎

2.2.
Basic constructions

Definition 2.10
.

Let
Q
and
N
be abelian groups, let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be an extended quadratic form over
Q
, and let
h:N\rightarrow M
be a homomorphism. The
pullback
of
\underline{M}
by
h
is

h^{*}(\underline{M})=(N,h^{*}\lambda,h^{*}\mu)

where
h^{*}\lambda(x,y)=\lambda(h(x),h(y))
and
h^{*}\mu=\mu\circ h
, this is also an extended quadratic form over
Q
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
be extended quadratic forms over some abelian group
Q
. A
morphism

h:\underline{N}\rightarrow\underline{M}
of extended quadratic forms is a homomorphism
h:N\rightarrow M
such that
\underline{N}=h^{*}(\underline{M})
.

If
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda_{M},\mu_{M})
and
\underline{N}=(N,\lambda_{N},\mu_{N})
, then
\underline{N}=h^{*}(\underline{M})
if and only if
\lambda_{M}(h(x),h(y))=\lambda_{N}(x,y)
and
\mu_{M}(h(x))=\mu_{N}(x)
for every
x,y\in N
.

Composition of morphisms can be defined in the obvious way, and extended quadratic forms over a fixed group
Q
together with the above defined morphisms form a category. In this category a morphism
h:\underline{N}\rightarrow\underline{M}
is an isomorphism if and only if the underlying homomorphism
h:N\rightarrow M
is an isomorphism.

Definition 2.12
.

The
standard rank-
2k
hyperbolic form
is
\underline{H}_{2k}=(\mathbb{Z}^{2k},\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}0&I_{k}\\
I_{k}&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr],0)
.

Since the third component of
\underline{H}_{2k}
is zero, it can be regarded as an extended quadratic form over any abelian group
Q
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be an extended quadratic form over some abelian group
Q
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\underline{M}
is hyperbolic.

(2)

\underline{M}
is free, metabolic, even and
\mu=0
.

(3)

\underline{M}
is isomorphic to
\underline{H}_{2k}
, where
2k=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\underline{M}
.

Proof.

\eqref{el:he1}\Rightarrow\eqref{el:he2}\Rightarrow\eqref{el:he3}\Rightarrow\eqref{el:he1}
. The
\eqref{el:he2}\Rightarrow\eqref{el:he3}
step follows from Lemma
2.9
.
∎

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda_{M},\mu_{M})
and
\underline{N}=(N,\lambda_{N},\mu_{N})
be extended quadratic forms over some abelian group
Q
. Their
direct sum
is

\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N}=(M\oplus N,\lambda_{M}\oplus\lambda_{N},\mu_{M}\oplus\mu_{N})

where
(\lambda_{M}\oplus\lambda_{N})((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))=\lambda_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})+\lambda_{N}(y_{1},y_{2})
and
(\mu_{M}\oplus\mu_{N})(x,y)=\mu_{M}(x)+\mu_{N}(y)
, this is also an extended quadratic form over
Q
.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be an extended quadratic form over some abelian group
Q
. We define

\displaystyle-\underline{M}

\displaystyle=(M,-\lambda,-\mu)

\displaystyle\underline{M}{}^{*}

\displaystyle=(M,\lambda,-\mu)

where
(-\lambda)(x,y)=-\lambda(x,y)
and
(-\mu)(x)=-\mu(x)
.

If
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
are free/nonsingular/metabolic/hyperbolic/even/geometric (with respect to some homomorphism), then the same property also holds for
\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N}
,
-\underline{M}
and
\underline{M}{}^{*}
. If
\underline{M}
is full, then
\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N}
,
-\underline{M}
and
\underline{M}{}^{*}
are full too.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
I:A\rightarrow B
be an isomorphism of abelian groups. We define the
diagonal
and
anti-diagonal with respect to
I
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{I}

\displaystyle=\{(x,I(x))\mid x\in A\}\leq A\oplus B

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}_{I}

\displaystyle=\{(x,-I(x))\mid x\in A\}\leq A\oplus B

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda_{M},\mu_{M})
and
\underline{N}=(N,\lambda_{N},\mu_{N})
be free extended quadratic forms over some abelian group
Q
and let
I:\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{N}
be an isomorphism. Then

a)
\Delta_{I}
is a lagrangian in
\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{N})
.

b)
\Delta^{*}_{I}
is a lagrangian in
\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{N}{}^{*})
.
∎

Verifying that
\Delta_{I}
and
\Delta^{*}_{I}
have the required properties is straightforward; or see
[
23
, Lemma 4.1.22]
. See also
[
23
, Lemmas 4.1.23-25]
for more detailed proofs of Lemmas
2.18
-
2.20
below.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be an extended quadratic form. The homomorphism
-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits:\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{M}{}^{*}
is an isomorphism of extended quadratic forms.
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be a free metabolic form, and let
L\leq M
be a lagrangian. There is an isomorphism
J:\underline{M}\rightarrow-\underline{M}
such that
J\big|_{L}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{L}
.

Proof.

Let
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k},f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{k}
be a basis of
M
given by Lemma
2.9
, and let
d_{i}=\lambda(f_{i},f_{i})
(so
d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{k}
are the diagonal entries of
D
). Let
f^{\prime}_{i}=d_{i}e_{i}-f_{i}
, and define
J:M\rightarrow M
by setting
J(e_{i})=e_{i}
and
J(f_{i})=f^{\prime}_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
be a free metabolic form of rank
2k
, and let
L\leq M
be a lagrangian. There is an isomorphism
I:\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
such that
I(L\oplus L)=L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
.

Proof.

Let
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k},f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{k}
be a basis of
M
given by Lemma
2.9
, and let
d_{i}=\lambda(f_{i},f_{i})
. We will also use the notation
e_{i}
,
f_{i}
for the basis of the first component in
M\oplus M
and
M\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2k}
, and the basis of the second component of
M\oplus M
will be denoted by
\bar{e}_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
. Let
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k},b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{k}
denote the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{2k}
. With this notation let
I(e_{i})=e_{i}+b_{i}
,
I(f_{i})=f_{i}+d_{i}b_{i}
,
I(\bar{e}_{i})=-b_{i}
and
I(\bar{f}_{i})=f_{i}-a_{i}
.
∎

2.3.
Full geometric metabolic forms

In this section we prove Theorem
2.22
, which states that free full geometric metabolic forms are unique up to stable isomorphism. We will need the following lemma, part a) of which can be extracted from the proof of
[
20
, Proposition 8. ii)]
.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
F
,
G
and
A
be abelian groups, and suppose that
F
and
G
are free. Let
f:F\rightarrow A
and
g:G\rightarrow A
be surjective homomorphisms. Then

a) There exist free abelian groups
F^{\prime}
,
G^{\prime}
, and an isomorphism
h:F\oplus F^{\prime}\rightarrow G\oplus G^{\prime}
such that
(f+0)=(g+0)\circ h:F\oplus F^{\prime}\rightarrow A
.

b) If, in addition,
A
is free and
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits F=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits G
, then there is an isomorphism
h:F\rightarrow G
such that
f=g\circ h
.

Proof.

a) Since
F
is free and
g
is surjective, the homomorphism
f
can be lifted to a homomorphism
\bar{f}:F\rightarrow G
such that
f=g\circ\bar{f}
. Let
\bar{f}_{0}:F_{0}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits g\subseteq G
be a surjective homomorphism from some free abelian group
F_{0}
to
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits g
. Let
F_{1}=F\oplus F_{0}
and
\bar{f}_{1}=\bar{f}+\bar{f}_{0}:F_{1}=F\oplus F_{0}\rightarrow G
.

We prove that
\bar{f}_{1}
is surjective. Let
y\in G
be an arbitrary element, then there is an
x\in F
such that
f(x)=g(y)
(because
f
is surjective). Let
y_{0}=\bar{f}(x)\in\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits\bar{f}\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{1}
, then
y-y_{0}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits g=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{0}\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{1}
, therefore
y=y_{0}+(y-y_{0})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{1}
.

Since
G
is free,
\bar{f}_{1}
has a right inverse, ie. there is a homomorphism
c:G\rightarrow F_{1}
such that
\bar{f}_{1}\circ c=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{G}
. This also implies that
F_{1}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{1}\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits c\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{1}\oplus G
.

Let
F^{\prime}=F_{0}\oplus G
and
G^{\prime}=F_{1}
. Then
F\oplus F^{\prime}=F\oplus(F_{0}\oplus G)\cong F_{1}\oplus G
and
G\oplus G^{\prime}=G\oplus F_{1}
, and we can define the homomorphism
h:F_{1}\oplus G\rightarrow G\oplus F_{1}
by the formula
h(x,y)=(\bar{f}_{1}(x),x+c(y))
.

To show that
h
is an isomorphism, we use the decomposition
F_{1}\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{1}\oplus G
. We have
F\oplus F^{\prime}\cong(\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{1}\oplus G)\oplus G
and
G\oplus G^{\prime}\cong G\oplus(\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{1}\oplus G)
, and
h
is given by the formula
h((x_{1},y_{2}),y)=(y_{2},(x_{1},y_{2}+y))
, therefore it is an isomorphism.

Finally, for any
x\in F
,
x_{0}\in F_{0}
and
y\in G
we have
(g+0)\circ h((x,x_{0}),y)=g\circ\bar{f}_{1}(x,x_{0})=g\circ(\bar{f}+\bar{f}_{0})(x,x_{0})=g\circ\bar{f}(x)=f(x)=(f+0)(x,(x_{0},y))
, where we used that
g\circ\bar{f}_{0}=0
, because
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits\bar{f}_{0}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits g
. Therefore
(f+0)=(g+0)\circ h
.

b) Since
A
is free, there are maps
c:A\rightarrow F
and
d:A\rightarrow G
such that
f\circ c=g\circ d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{A}
. We get the decompositions
F=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits f\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits c
and
G=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits g\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits d
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits c\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits d\cong A
. The subgroups
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits f
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits g
are free, and
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits f=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits F-\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits G-\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits g
, therefore there is an isomorphism
h_{0}:\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits f\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits g
. Then we can take
h=h_{0}\oplus(d\circ f)\big|_{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits c}:F=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits f\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits c\rightarrow G=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits g\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits d
.
∎

Theorem 2.22
.

Let
Q
be an abelian group and
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
a homomorphism. Let
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
and
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}=(M^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be extended quadratic forms over
Q
. Suppose that they are metabolic, free, full and geometric (with respect to
v
), and let
L\leq M
and
L^{\prime}\leq M^{\prime}
be lagrangians. Then

a) For some
k,l\geq 0
there is an isomorphism
I:\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}\rightarrow\underline{M}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H}_{2l}
such that
I(L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))=L^{\prime}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l})
.

b) If, in addition,
Q
is free and
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\underline{M}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
, then there is an isomorphism
I:\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
such that
I(L)=L^{\prime}
.

Proof.

a) Since
L\leq M
and
L^{\prime}\leq M^{\prime}
are direct summands, they have direct complements
N\leq M
and
N^{\prime}\leq M^{\prime}
. The homomorphism
\mu:M=L\oplus N\rightarrow Q
is surjective, and
\mu\big|_{L}=0
, therefore
\mu\big|_{N}:N\rightarrow Q
is surjective. Similarly,
\mu^{\prime}\big|_{N^{\prime}}:N^{\prime}\rightarrow Q
is surjective too. By Lemma
2.21
a) there are free abelian groups
A,A^{\prime}
and an isomorphism
h:N\oplus A\rightarrow N^{\prime}\oplus A^{\prime}
such that
(\mu\big|_{N}+0)=(\mu^{\prime}\big|_{N^{\prime}}+0)\circ h:N\oplus A\rightarrow Q
. Let
k=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A
and
l=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A^{\prime}
.

Let
\underline{\tilde{M}}=(\tilde{M},\tilde{\lambda},\tilde{\mu})
denote the direct sum
\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
, that is
\tilde{M}=M\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2k}
,
\tilde{\lambda}=\lambda\oplus\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}0&I_{k}\\
I_{k}&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]
and
\tilde{\mu}=\mu\oplus 0
. Similarly let
\underline{\tilde{M}}{}^{\prime}=(\tilde{M}^{\prime},\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}^{\prime})=\underline{M}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H}_{2l}
. Let
\tilde{L}=L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
and
\tilde{L}^{\prime}=L^{\prime}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l})
, these are lagrangians in
\underline{\tilde{M}}
and
\underline{\tilde{M}}{}^{\prime}
respectively. Hence the new extended quadratic forms
\underline{\tilde{M}}
and
\underline{\tilde{M}}{}^{\prime}
are again metabolic, free, full and geometric. Let
\tilde{N}=N\oplus(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\})
and
\tilde{N}^{\prime}=N^{\prime}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}^{l}\times\{0\})
, then
\tilde{M}=\tilde{L}\oplus\tilde{N}
and
\tilde{M}^{\prime}=\tilde{L}^{\prime}\oplus\tilde{N}^{\prime}
. If we fix some isomorphisms
A\cong\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\}
and
A^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{l}\times\{0\}
, then
h
determines an isomorphism
\tilde{h}:\tilde{N}\rightarrow\tilde{N}^{\prime}
such that
\tilde{\mu}\big|_{\tilde{N}}=\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}\big|_{\tilde{N}^{\prime}}\circ\tilde{h}
.

Let
r=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\tilde{N}(=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\tilde{N}^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\tilde{L}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\tilde{L}^{\prime})
. Let
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{r}
be a basis of
\tilde{N}
, and let
f^{\prime}_{i}=\tilde{h}(f_{i})
. By Lemma
2.8

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits\lambda
determines an isomorphism
\tilde{N}\cong\tilde{L}^{*}
, so the basis
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{r}
of
\tilde{N}\cong\tilde{L}^{*}
determines a dual basis
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{r}
of
\tilde{L}
(hence
\tilde{\lambda}(e_{i},f_{j})=0
if
i\neq j
and
\tilde{\lambda}(e_{i},f_{i})=1
). Similarly,
\tilde{L}^{\prime}
has a basis
e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{r}
which is dual to the basis
f^{\prime}_{1},f^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{r}
of
\tilde{N}^{\prime}
.

Next we define the elements
\bar{f}_{1},\bar{f}_{2},\ldots,\bar{f}_{r}\in\tilde{M}
recursively. Let
\bar{f}_{1}=f_{1}
, and let
\bar{f}_{i+1}=f_{i+1}-\sum_{j=1}^{i}\tilde{\lambda}(\bar{f}_{j},f_{i+1})e_{j}-\left\lfloor\frac{\tilde{\lambda}(f_{i+1},f_{i+1})}{2}\right\rfloor e_{i+1}
. By induction we see that
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{r},\bar{f}_{1},\bar{f}_{2},\ldots,\bar{f}_{i}
is a basis of
\tilde{L}\oplus\left<f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{i}\right>
, therefore
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{r},\bar{f}_{1},\allowbreak\bar{f}_{2},\ldots,\bar{f}_{r}
is a basis of
\tilde{M}
. By construction
\tilde{\lambda}(e_{i},\bar{f}_{j})=\tilde{\lambda}(e_{i},f_{j})
for every
i,j
,
\tilde{\lambda}(\bar{f}_{i},\bar{f}_{j})=0
if
i\neq j
, and
\tilde{\lambda}(\bar{f}_{i},\bar{f}_{i})=0
if
\tilde{\lambda}(f_{i},f_{i})
is even and
\tilde{\lambda}(\bar{f}_{i},\bar{f}_{i})=1
if
\tilde{\lambda}(f_{i},f_{i})
is odd. Furthermore
\tilde{\mu}(\bar{f}_{i})=\tilde{\mu}(f_{i})
. We define the elements
\bar{f}^{\prime}_{1},\bar{f}^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,\bar{f}^{\prime}_{r}\in\tilde{M}^{\prime}
analogously.

Let
I:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{M}^{\prime}
be the homomorphism given by
I(e_{i})=e^{\prime}_{i}
and
I(\bar{f}_{i})=\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i}
. This is an isomorphism of abelian groups, and
I(\tilde{L})=\tilde{L}^{\prime}
. We need to check that
I
is a morphism of extended quadratic forms. For every
i
we have
\tilde{\mu}(e_{i})=0=\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}(e^{\prime}_{i})
and
\tilde{\mu}(\bar{f}_{i})=\tilde{\mu}(f_{i})=\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}\circ\tilde{h}(f_{i})=\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}(f^{\prime}_{i})=\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}(\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i})
, therefore
\tilde{\mu}=\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}\circ I
. For every
i,j

\tilde{\lambda}(e_{i},e_{j})=0=\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}(e^{\prime}_{i},e^{\prime}_{j})
. If
i\neq j
, then
\tilde{\lambda}(e_{i},\bar{f}_{j})=0=\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}(e^{\prime}_{i},\bar{f}^{\prime}_{j})
and
\tilde{\lambda}(\bar{f}_{i},\bar{f}_{j})=0=\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}(\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i},\bar{f}^{\prime}_{j})
. For every
i
we have
\tilde{\lambda}(e_{i},\bar{f}_{i})=1=\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}(e^{\prime}_{i},\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i})
. Finally, both
\tilde{\lambda}(\bar{f}_{i},\bar{f}_{i})
and
\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}(\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i},\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i})
are either
0
or
1
. And since
\underline{\tilde{M}}
and
\underline{\tilde{M}}{}^{\prime}
are geometric,
\varrho_{2}\circ\tilde{\lambda}(\bar{f}_{i},\bar{f}_{i})=v\circ\tilde{\mu}(\bar{f}_{i})=v\circ\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}\circ I(\bar{f}_{i})=v\circ\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}(\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i})=\varrho_{2}\circ\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}(\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i},\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i})
, so this means that
\tilde{\lambda}(\bar{f}_{i},\bar{f}_{i})=\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}(\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i},\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i})
. Therefore
\tilde{\lambda}(x,y)=\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}(I(x),I(y))
for every
x,y\in\tilde{M}
, so
I
is an isomorphism of extended quadratic forms.

b) In this case we can use part b) of Lemma
2.21
instead of part a). Hence we can take
A=A^{\prime}=0
, so the statement of part a) holds with
k=l=0
.
∎

An immediate corollary is the following:

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
\underline{H}
and
\underline{H}{}^{\prime}
be hyperbolic extended quadratic forms over some abelian group
Q
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\underline{H}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits\underline{H}{}^{\prime}
. Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be lagrangians in
\underline{H}
and
\underline{H}{}^{\prime}
respectively. Then there is an isomorphism
I:\underline{H}\rightarrow\underline{H}{}^{\prime}
such that
I(L)=L^{\prime}
.

Proof.

\underline{H}
and
\underline{H}{}^{\prime}
can be regarded as extended quadratic forms over
0
(see Lemma
2.13
). Over
0
they are full and geometric (with respect to the trivial homomorphism). Since hyperbolic forms are also free and metabolic by definition, and
0
is free, we can apply Theorem
2.22
b) to get an isomorphism
I:\underline{H}\rightarrow\underline{H}{}^{\prime}
such that
I(L)=L^{\prime}
. This
I
is also an isomorphism between
\underline{H}
and
\underline{H}{}^{\prime}
when they are regarded as extended quadratic forms over
Q
.
∎

3.
The group
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M},L)

Fix an abelian group
Q
with a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. In this section every extended quadratic form we consider will be over
Q
, and will be assumed to be free and geometric (with respect to
v
).

For a metabolic form
\underline{M}
and a lagrangian
L
we introduce the group
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)
, and its stable version
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M},L)
. Then we show that
\Phi\oplus\Phi^{-1}\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M},L\oplus L)
for any
\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(\underline{M})
, which will play a key role in the proof of Theorem
4.24
.

Definition 3.1
.

Given a metabolic form
\underline{M}
and a lagrangian
L
in
\underline{M}
, we define the subgroup
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(\underline{M})
, which is generated by the following elements:

•

Automorphisms
f:\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{M}
such that
f(L)=L
.

•

I^{-1}\circ(\sigma\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M^{\prime}})\circ I
for every isomorphism
I:\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{H}_{2}\oplus\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
(for some metabolic form
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
) such that
I(L)=(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z})\oplus L^{\prime}
for some lagrangian
L^{\prime}
in
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
, where
\sigma:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is the flip map (ie.
\sigma(a,b)=(b,a)
).

Definition 3.2
.

Given a metabolic form
\underline{M}
and a lagrangian
L
in
\underline{M}
, let

\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M},L)=\{f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(\underline{M})\mid\exists(\underline{N},K):f\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{N}\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N},L\oplus K)\}

where
\underline{N}
denotes some metabolic form with a lagrangian
K
.

If
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)
, then
f\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{N}\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N},L\oplus K)
for any
(\underline{N},K)
, showing that
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M},L)
is a subgroup of
\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(\underline{M})
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M},L)
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose that
\underline{M}
and
\underline{N}
are metabolic forms with lagrangians
L
and
K
respectively. If
F:\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{N}
is an isomorphism such that
F(L)=K
, then
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)=F^{-1}\circ\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{N},K)\circ F
.

Proof.

Suppose that
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(\underline{N})
. Then
f
is a generator of
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{N},K)
if and only if
F^{-1}\circ f\circ F
is a generator of
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)
.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose that
\underline{M}
is a metabolic form, and
L
and
K
are lagrangians in
\underline{M}
. If there is an
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)
such that
f(L)=K
, then
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)=\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},K)
.

Proof.

We have
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},K)=f\circ\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)\circ f^{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)
by Lemma
3.3
and because
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)
.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\underline{M}
be an extended quadratic form and
\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(\underline{M})
. Then we have
\Phi\oplus\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M}),\Delta_{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits})
.

Proof.

Since
\Delta_{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
is the diagonal,
(\Phi\oplus\Phi)(\Delta_{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits})=\Delta_{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\underline{M}
be a metabolic form and
L
a lagrangian in
\underline{M}
. Then

\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M}),\Delta_{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits})=\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M}),L\oplus L)\text{\,.}

Proof.

Denote the rank of
\underline{M}
by
2k
. Let
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k},f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{k}
be a basis of
M
given by Lemma
2.9
, and let
d_{i}=\lambda(f_{i},f_{i})
. We will use the notation
e_{i}
,
f_{i}
when
M
is regarded as the underlying abelian group of
\underline{M}
, and the same elements will be denoted by
\bar{e}_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
when
M
is regarded as the underlying abelian group of
-\underline{M}
. Let
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k},b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{k}
denote the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{2k}
. Let
I:\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
and
J:\underline{M}\rightarrow-\underline{M}
denote the isomorphisms from Lemmas
2.20
and
2.19
respectively.

The composition of the flip map
\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M})\rightarrow(-\underline{M})\oplus\underline{M}
,
J^{-1}\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M}
and
I
is an isomorphism
F:\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M})\rightarrow\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
given by
F(e_{i})=-b_{i}
,
F(f_{i})=f_{i}-a_{i}
,
F(\bar{e}_{i})=e_{i}+b_{i}
and
F(\bar{f}_{i})=d_{i}e_{i}-f_{i}
. Therefore
F(L\oplus L)=L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
and
F(\Delta_{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits})=L\oplus(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\})
.

If
\Sigma:\mathbb{Z}^{2k}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2k}
denotes the flip map (ie.
\Sigma(a,b)=(b,a)
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
), then
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M}\oplus\Sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k},L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))
. Since
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M}\oplus\Sigma)(L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))=L\oplus(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\})
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k},L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))=\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k},L\oplus(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\}))
by Lemma
3.4
. Therefore

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M}),\Delta_{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits})

\displaystyle=F^{-1}\circ\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k},L\oplus(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\}))\circ F=

\displaystyle=F^{-1}\circ\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k},L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))\circ F=

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M}),L\oplus L)

by Lemma
3.3
.
∎

The following is a generalisation of Wall’s
[
29
, Lemma 6.2]
.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
\underline{M}
be a metabolic form, and
L
a lagrangian in
\underline{M}
. For any
\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}\nolimits(\underline{M})
we have

\Phi\oplus\Phi^{-1}\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M},L\oplus L)\text{\,.}

Proof.

By Lemma
3.5
and Proposition
3.6
we have
\Phi\oplus\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M}),L\oplus L)
. This implies that

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M}\oplus\Phi\oplus\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M}),L\oplus L\oplus L)

and that

\Phi\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M}\oplus\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M}),L\oplus L\oplus L)

By composing the latter with the inverse of the former we get that

\Phi\oplus\Phi^{-1}\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M}\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M}\oplus(-\underline{M}),L\oplus L\oplus L)

hence
\Phi\oplus\Phi^{-1}\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M},L\oplus L)
.
∎

4.
Extended
\ell
-monoids

In this section we will define and study extended
\ell
-monoids.

Recall that in the setting of Kreck
[
20
]
the monoid
l_{2q+1}(e)
consists of equivalence classes of pairs
(\underline{H}_{2k},V)
, where
V
is a half-rank direct summand in
\underline{H}_{2k}
. Crowley and Sixt
[
10
]
gave an equivalent definition in terms of quasi-formations: triples
(\underline{M},L,V)
consisting of an (ordinary) quadratic form
\underline{M}
, a lagrangian
L
and a half-rank direct summand
V
.

Building on these ideas, we define extended
\ell
-monoids. First we define quasi-formations (over an abelian group
Q
), using extended quadratic forms over
Q
instead of ordinary quadratic forms. An extended
\ell
-monoid will then consist of equivalence classes of quasi-formations. As our goal is to define an obstruction in this
\ell
-monoid (see the definitions of
\theta_{W,F}
and
[\theta_{W,F}]
in Section
6
), we define the equivalence relation in a way which will be compatible with the effect of surgery on
\theta_{W,F}
. Because of this, the relation used in
[
10
, Definition 3.8 (i)]
will instead be a theorem in our setting (see Theorem
4.24
).

For most of this section we will work in a purely algebraic setting. First we define the
\ell
-monoid associated to a group
Q
and a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. We will consider two versions. In the first version elements are represented by quasi-formations such that the torsion subgroup of the abelian group underlying the extended quadratic form is isomorphic to a fixed torsion group
R
, we will denote this by
\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
. In the second version (which is a special case of the first), denoted by
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, the extended quadratic forms are free. The second version is more suitable for computations, as it has a natural monoid structure given by direct sum (see Proposition
4.20
). However, the surgery obstruction that we will define in Section
6
lives in an
\ell
-monoid of the first type. We will prove that the two versions are equivalent, ie. there is a natural bijection between them (see Proposition
4.13
), this will allow us to perform computations with the surgery obstruction.

Next we prove the important identity of Theorem
4.24
. Then we define the group
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, which is a generalisation of the classical L-group
L_{2q+1}
. It is the group of invertible elements of
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, and we show (using the fact that
L_{2q+1}\cong 0
) that if
Q
is free, then
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)\cong 0
. We will end the section by defining the
\ell
-monoid associated to a space
B
with a stable bundle
\xi
over it.

4.1.
Definitions

Definition 4.1
.

A
quasi-formation
over an abelian group
Q
is a triple

(\underline{M};L,V)

where

•

\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
is a metabolic extended quadratic form over
Q

•

L\leq M
is a T-lagrangian

•

V\leq M
is a free half-rank direct summand

Definition 4.2
.

A quasi-formation
(\underline{M};L,V)
is called
elementary
, if
M=L\oplus V
(as an internal direct sum).

Definition 4.3
.

The
standard rank-
2k
elementary hyperbolic quasi-formation
is
\mathcal{H}_{2k}=(\underline{H}_{2k};\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k},\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\})
.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
(\underline{M};L,V)
and
(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})
be quasi-formations over an abelian group
Q
. They are
isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism
h:\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
such that
L^{\prime}=h(L)
and
V^{\prime}=h(V)
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
(\underline{M};L,V)
and
(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})
be quasi-formations over an abelian group
Q
. Their
direct sum
is
(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})=(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L\oplus L^{\prime},V\oplus V^{\prime})
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(\underline{M};L,V)
and
(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})
be quasi-formations over an abelian group
Q
. They are
stably isomorphic
if there are integers
k,l\geq 0
such that
(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2k}\cong(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2l}
. Stable isomorphism will be denoted by
{}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}{}
.

Definition 4.7
.

For an abelian group
Q
with a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, a torsion abelian group
R
and an even integer
q\geq 0
let

\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)

denote the class of quasi-formations
(\underline{M};L,V)
over
Q
such that
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
is geometric (with respect to
v
), full and
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits M\cong R
.

Note that the class
\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
does not depend on the choice of
q
.

Definition 4.8
.

For an abelian group
Q
with a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, a torsion abelian group
R
and an even integer
q\geq 0
let

{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)=\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)/{\cong}

denote the set of isomorphism classes of quasi-formations
(\underline{M};L,V)
over
Q
such that
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
is geometric (with respect to
v
), full and
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits M\cong R
.

Definition 4.9
.

For an abelian group
Q
with a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, a torsion abelian group
R
and an even integer
q\geq 0
let

{s}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)=\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)/{\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}}

denote the set of stable isomorphism classes of quasi-formations
(\underline{M};L,V)
over
Q
such that
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
is geometric (with respect to
v
), full and
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits M\cong R
.

Definition 4.10
.

For an abelian group
Q
with a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, a torsion abelian group
R
and an even integer
q\geq 0
let

\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)=\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)/{\sim}

be the set of quasi-formations
(\underline{M};L,V)
over
Q
such that
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
is geometric (with respect to
v
), full and
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits M\cong R
up to the equivalence relation
\sim
, which is generated by the following elementary equivalences:

•

(\underline{M};L,V)\sim(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})
if
(\underline{M};L,V)\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})

•

(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),V)\sim(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}),V)
, where
L
is a T-lagrangian in
\underline{M}
and
V
is a free half-rank direct summand in
\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2}

The equivalence class of
(\underline{M};L,V)
will be denoted by
[\underline{M};L,V]
.

Definition 4.11
.

For an abelian group
Q
with a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and an even integer
q\geq 0
let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,0)\text{\,,}

\displaystyle{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)

\displaystyle={g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,0)\text{\,,}

\displaystyle{s}_{2q+1}(Q,v)

\displaystyle={s}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,0)\text{\,,}

\displaystyle\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)

\displaystyle=\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,0)\text{\,.}

To prove the equivalence of
\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
and
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
we will need the following notation:

Definition 4.12
.

Suppose that
\underline{N}=(N,\lambda_{N},\mu_{N})
is an extended quadratic form over an abelian group
Q
such that
\mu_{N}\big|_{\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N}=0
(note that this condition automatically holds if
Q
is free). Let
\bar{N}
denote
N/\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N
and
\pi_{N}:N\rightarrow N/\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N
the quotient map. Let
\bar{\lambda}_{N}
and
\bar{\mu}_{N}
denote the induced bilinear function and homomorphism on
\bar{N}
, and let
\underline{\bar{N}}=(\bar{N},\bar{\lambda}_{N},\bar{\mu}_{N})
. If
X\leq N
is a subgroup, then
\bar{X}
will denote the subgroup
\pi_{N}(X)\leq N/\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N
.

Note that
\pi_{N}:\underline{N}\rightarrow\underline{\bar{N}}
is a morphism of extended quadratic forms. If
\underline{N}
is nonsingular, metabolic, full or geometric (with respect to some homomorphism), then the same property also holds for
\underline{\bar{N}}
.

Proposition 4.13
.

For any abelian group
Q
with a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, torsion abelian group
R
and even integer
q\geq 0
, there is a canonical bijection between
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
and
\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
.

Proof.

First we define a function
F:\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)\rightarrow\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
. Let
\underline{R}=(R,0,0)
, it is a metabolic extended quadratic form of rank
0
over any group, geometric with respect to any homomorphism. If
(\underline{M};L,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, then
(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{R};R,0)\in\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
, let
F([\underline{M};L,V])=[\underline{M}\oplus\underline{R};L\oplus R,V\oplus 0]
.

Next we define a function
G:\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)\rightarrow\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. Suppose that
(\underline{N};K,W)\in\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
, where
\underline{N}=(N,\lambda,\mu)
. Since
\mu\big|_{\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N}=0
(because
\underline{N}
has a T-lagrangian), we can define
\underline{\bar{N}}
. Moreover,
\bar{K}=K/\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N
is a lagrangian in
\underline{\bar{N}}
, and
\bar{W}=(W\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N)/\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N
is a half-rank direct summand, hence
(\underline{\bar{N}};\bar{K},\bar{W})\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. Let
G([\underline{N};K,W])=[\underline{\bar{N}};\bar{K},\bar{W}]
.

It is immediate from the definitions that
G\circ F=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)}
. For the other composition, let
[\underline{N};K,W]\in\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
, then
F\circ G([\underline{N};K,W])=[\underline{\bar{N}}\oplus\underline{R};\bar{K}\oplus R,\bar{W}\oplus 0]
. Since
W
is a free direct summand,
\pi_{N}
has a right inverse
I_{0}:\bar{N}\rightarrow N
such that
W\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits I_{0}
. By choosing an isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N\cong R
we can extend
I_{0}
to an isomorphism
I:\bar{N}\oplus R\rightarrow N
. This is also an isomorphism
\underline{\bar{N}}\oplus\underline{R}\cong\underline{N}
of extended quadratic forms, and by construction
I(\bar{W}\oplus 0)=I_{0}(\bar{W})=W
. Since
K
is a T-lagrangian, we have
K=(K\cap\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits I_{0})\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N
, so
I(\bar{K}\oplus R)=I_{0}(\bar{K})\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N=(K\cap\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits I_{0})\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits N=K
. This shows that
[\underline{\bar{N}}\oplus\underline{R};\bar{K}\oplus R,\bar{W}\oplus 0]=[\underline{N};K,W]
, hence
F\circ G=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)}
.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

The same proof shows that there are canonical bijections between
{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
and
{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
and between
{s}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
and
{s}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
.

Recall that a quasi-formation
(\underline{M};L,V)
is called elementary if
M=L\oplus V
. This property is invariant under stable isomorphism (because
(\underline{M};L,V)
is elementary if and only if
(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2k}
is elementary, for any
k
). Therefore an element of
{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
or
{s}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
has an elementary representative if and only if all of its representatives are elementary.

Definition 4.15
.

An element of
{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
or
{s}^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
is called
elementary
, if its representatives are elementary.

In contrast to the above, an elementary quasi-formation may be equivalent to a non-elementary one under the equivalence relation
\sim
(in fact every equivalence class contains non-elementary quasi-formations, see Lemma
4.19
below).

Definition 4.16
.

An element of
\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
is called
elementary
, if it has an elementary representative.

Remark 4.17
.

Under the bijection of Proposition
4.13
elementary elements of
\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)
correspond to elementary elements of
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)=\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,0)
.

4.2.
The monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)

From now on we will focus on the free version
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. We show that it has a natural monoid structure and, using the results on
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M},L)
from Section
3
, we prove Theorem
4.24
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Suppose that
(\underline{M};L,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. If
\Psi\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)
, then we have
(\underline{M};L,V)\sim(\underline{M};L,\Psi(V))
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the statement when
\Psi
is a generator of
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)
.

If
\Psi(L)=L
, then
\Psi
is an isomorphism between
(\underline{M};L,V)
and
(\underline{M};L,\Psi(V))
.

If there is an isomorphism
I:\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{H}_{2}\oplus\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
such that
I(L)=(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z})\oplus L^{\prime}
for some lagrangian
L^{\prime}
in
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
and
\Psi=I^{-1}\circ(\sigma\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M^{\prime}})\circ I
, then
I\circ\Psi(L)=(\sigma\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M^{\prime}})\circ I(L)=(\sigma\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M^{\prime}})((\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z})\oplus L^{\prime})=(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\})\oplus L^{\prime}
. So

\displaystyle(\underline{M};L,V)

\displaystyle\cong(\underline{H}_{2}\oplus\underline{M}{}^{\prime};I\circ\Psi(L),I\circ\Psi(V))=

\displaystyle=(\underline{H}_{2}\oplus\underline{M}{}^{\prime};(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\})\oplus L^{\prime},I\circ\Psi(V))\sim

\displaystyle\sim(\underline{H}_{2}\oplus\underline{M}{}^{\prime};(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z})\oplus L^{\prime},I\circ\Psi(V))=

\displaystyle=(\underline{H}_{2}\oplus\underline{M}{}^{\prime};I(L),I\circ\Psi(V))\cong

\displaystyle\cong(\underline{M};L,\Psi(V))

where we used the isomorphisms
I\circ\Psi
and
I
. Therefore the statement holds for every generator of
\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M},L)
.
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

If
(\underline{M};L,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
and
k\geq 0
, then we have
(\underline{M};L,V)\sim(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{H}_{2k};\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k},\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
.

Proof.

As in Proposition
3.6
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M}\oplus\Sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k},L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))
, hence by Lemma
4.18
,
(\underline{M};L,V)\sim(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{H}_{2k};\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k},\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\})\sim(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{H}_{2k};\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k},\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
.
∎

Proposition 4.20
.

a) Direct sum of quasi-formations induces an operation
\oplus
on
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, and
(\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v),\oplus)
is a commutative monoid.

b) If
(\underline{M};L,L)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, then
[\underline{M};L,L]
is the zero element in
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
.

Proof.

It is clear that there is a well-defined induced operation
\oplus
on
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, which is associative and commutative (cf.
[
23
, Proposition 4.2.18]
). We need to show that it has a zero element. (Note that
\underline{H}_{2k}
is not full if
Q\neq 0
, so
\mathcal{H}_{2k}\not\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
.) We will do this in 3 steps.

First, we show that there is a quasi-formation of the form
(\underline{M};L,L)
in the class
\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
(in particular
\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
and
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
are non-empty). Let
\mu_{0}:A\rightarrow Q
be a surjective homomorphism from some free abelian group
A
to
Q
. Let
k=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A
, and let
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{k}
be a basis of
A
. Let
L
be another rank-
k
free abelian group with basis
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k}
. Let
M=L\oplus A
. Let
\lambda:M\times M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the bilinear function given by the block matrix

\begin{bmatrix}0&I_{k}\\
I_{k}&D\end{bmatrix}

(in the basis
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k},f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{k}
), where
D
is a diagonal matrix such that the diagonal entry corresponding to
f_{i}
is
0
if
v\circ\mu_{0}(f_{i})=0
and
1
if
v\circ\mu_{0}(f_{i})=1
(hence
\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(f_{i},f_{i})=v\circ\mu_{0}(f_{i})
). Let
\mu:M\rightarrow Q
be the homomorphism such that
\mu\big|_{L}=0
and
\mu\big|_{A}=\mu_{0}
. Then
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
is an extended quadratic form over
Q
, and it is free and full. It is also geometric, because for any
x=\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}e_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}s_{i}f_{i}\in M
we have

\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(x,x)=\varrho_{2}\left(2\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}s_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}s_{i}^{2}\lambda(f_{i},f_{i})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varrho_{2}(s_{i}^{2})\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(f_{i},f_{i})=\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varrho_{2}(s_{i})\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(f_{i},f_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varrho_{2}(s_{i})v\circ\mu_{0}(f_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}v(s_{i}\mu_{0}(f_{i}))=v\circ\mu(x)

Furthermore,
L
is a lagrangian in
M
, therefore
(\underline{M};L,L)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
.

Second, we show that if
(\underline{M};L,L),(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, then
(\underline{M};L,L)\sim(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},L^{\prime})
. By Theorem
2.22
a) there is an isomorphism
I:\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}\rightarrow\underline{M}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H}_{2l}
such that
I(L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))=L^{\prime}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l})
for some
k,l\geq 0
. So we have

\displaystyle(\underline{M};L,L)

\displaystyle\sim(\underline{M};L,L)\oplus(\underline{H}_{2k};\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k},\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})=

\displaystyle=(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k};L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}),L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))\cong

\displaystyle\cong(\underline{M}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H}_{2l};L^{\prime}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}),L^{\prime}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}))=

\displaystyle=(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},L^{\prime})\oplus(\underline{H}_{2l};\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l},\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l})\sim

\displaystyle\sim(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},L^{\prime})

using Lemma
4.19
and the isomorphism
I
. Therefore all quasi-formations of the form
(\underline{M};L,L)
are in the same equivalence class, which we will denote by
\alpha\in\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
.

Third, we show that
\alpha
is a zero element in
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. Suppose that
(\underline{M};L,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, then
(\underline{M};L,L)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
and
[\underline{M};L,L]=\alpha
. Let
I:\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M}\rightarrow\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
be the isomorphism constructed in the proof of Lemma
2.20
, satisfying
I(L\oplus L)=L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
. Let
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}
be a basis of
V
, then
v_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(p_{ij}e_{j}+q_{ij}f_{j})
for some
p_{ij},q_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
(using the notation of Lemma
2.20
). We have
I(v_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(p_{ij}I(e_{j})+q_{ij}I(f_{j}))=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(p_{ij}(e_{j}+b_{j})+q_{ij}(f_{j}+d_{j}b_{j}))=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(p_{ij}e_{j}+q_{ij}f_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(p_{ij}+q_{ij}d_{j})b_{j}=v_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}(p_{ij}+q_{ij}d_{j})b_{j}
and
I(\bar{e}_{i})=-b_{i}
. Thus
I(\left<v_{i},\bar{e}_{i}\right>)=\left<v_{i},b_{i}\right>
, showing that
I(V\oplus L)=V\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
. Using the isomorphism
I
and Lemma
4.19
we get the following:

\displaystyle(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{M};L,L)

\displaystyle=(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{M};L\oplus L,V\oplus L)\cong

\displaystyle\cong(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k};L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}),V\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))=

\displaystyle=(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{H}_{2k};\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k},\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})\sim

\displaystyle\sim(\underline{M};L,V)

This means that
[\underline{M};L,V]\oplus\alpha=[\underline{M};L,V]
. Since this is true for any
[\underline{M};L,V]\in\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
,
\alpha
is a zero element (hence
the
zero element) in
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
.
∎

Remark 4.21
.

The direct sum of elementary quasi-formations is obviously elementary, hence the elementary elements of the monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
form a subsemigroup, which we denote by
\varepsilon\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, following Crowley-Sixt
[
10
]
. In general
\varepsilon\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
is not a submonoid of
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, because if
(\underline{M};L,V)
is equivalent to an elementary quasi-formation, then
\mu(V)=Q
, therefore
[\underline{M};L,L]\not\in\varepsilon\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
if
Q\neq 0
.

Lemma 4.22
.

Suppose that
(\underline{M};L,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
,
\underline{N}
is a free geometric (with respect to
v
) metabolic form over
Q
and
K
is a lagrangian in
\underline{N}
(so that
(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{N};K,K)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
). Then
(\underline{M};L,V)\sim(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{N};K,K)
.

Proof.

We have
(\underline{M};L,L)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
and by Proposition
4.20
b) it represents the zero element in
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
(note that if
\underline{N}
is not full, then
(\underline{N};K,K)\not\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
). The same is true for
