(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N};L\oplus K,L\oplus K)=(\underline{M};L,L)\oplus(\underline{N};K,K)
. Therefore we have
(\underline{M};L,V)\sim(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus((\underline{M};L,L)\oplus(\underline{N};K,K))\cong((\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{N};K,K))\oplus(\underline{M};L,L)\sim(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{N};K,K)
.
∎

Lemma 4.23
.

Suppose that
(\underline{M};L,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. If
\Psi\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{M},L)
, then

(\underline{M};L,V)\sim(\underline{M};L,\Psi(V))\text{\,.}

Proof.

By Definition
3.2
there is a metabolic form
\underline{N}
with a lagrangian
K
such that
\Psi\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{N}\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N},L\oplus K)
. By Lemma
4.22
it is enough to show that
(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N};L\oplus K,V\oplus K)\sim(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{N};L\oplus K,\Psi(V)\oplus K)
, which holds by Lemma
4.18
.
∎

Theorem 4.24
.

Suppose that
(\underline{M};L,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, and let
K
be a lagrangian in
\underline{M}
(so
(\underline{M};K,L),(\underline{M};K,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
). Then

[\underline{M};K,L]\oplus[\underline{M};L,V]=[\underline{M};K,V]\in\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)\text{\,.}

Proof.

By Theorem
2.22
a) there is an isomorphism
\Phi:\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}\rightarrow\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
such that
\Phi(L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}))=K\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
for some
k\geq 0
. Let
\underline{\tilde{M}}=\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2k}
,
\tilde{L}=L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
,
\tilde{K}=K\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
and
\tilde{V}=V\oplus(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\})
, then
[\underline{M};L,V]=[\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{L},\tilde{V}]
,
[\underline{M};K,V]=[\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K},\tilde{V}]
and (by Lemma
4.19
)
[\underline{M};K,L]=[\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K},\tilde{L}]
.

By Theorem
3.7

\Phi\oplus\Phi^{-1}\in\mathop{\mathrm{RU}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{st}}\nolimits}(\underline{\tilde{M}}\oplus\underline{\tilde{M}},\tilde{K}\oplus\tilde{K})
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle(\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K},\tilde{L})\oplus(\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{L},\tilde{V})

\displaystyle\cong(\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K},\tilde{L})\oplus(\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K},\Phi(\tilde{V}))=

\displaystyle=(\underline{\tilde{M}}\oplus\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K}\oplus\tilde{K},\tilde{L}\oplus\Phi(\tilde{V}))\sim

\displaystyle\sim(\underline{\tilde{M}}\oplus\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K}\oplus\tilde{K},\tilde{K}\oplus\tilde{V})=

\displaystyle=(\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K},\tilde{K})\oplus(\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K},\tilde{V})\sim

\displaystyle\sim(\underline{\tilde{M}};\tilde{K},\tilde{V})

using the isomorphism
\Phi
and Lemmas
4.23
and
4.22
.
∎

4.3.
The group
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)

In this section we consider the extended version of the L-groups.

Definition 4.25
.

For an abelian group
Q
with a homomorphism
v:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, a torsion abelian group
R
and an even integer
q\geq 0
let

L^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)=\{[\underline{M};L,K]\in\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,R)\mid\text{$K$ is a free lagrangian in $\underline{M}$}\}

and

L_{2q+1}(Q,v)=L^{T}_{2q+1}(Q,v,0)

Proposition 4.26
.

L_{2q+1}(Q,v)
is the group of invertible elements of
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
.

Proof.

First we show that every element of
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)
has an inverse. If
[\underline{M};L,K]\in L_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, then
K
is a lagrangian in
\underline{M}
, so
(\underline{M};K,L)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. By Theorem
4.24

[\underline{M};L,K]\oplus[\underline{M};K,L]=[\underline{M};L,L]
, which is the zero element, therefore
[\underline{M};K,L]
is the inverse of
[\underline{M};L,K]
.

Next we show that every invertible element is contained in
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. For an
(\underline{M};L,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
let
\alpha(\underline{M};L,V)
denote the subgroup
\mu(V)\leq Q
(where
\underline{M}=(M,\lambda,\mu)
). Then
\alpha(\underline{M};L,V)
is invariant under the elementary equivalences that define
\sim
, so it induces a well-defined function on
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. Moreover, we have
\alpha([\underline{M};L,V]\oplus[\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime}])=\alpha([\underline{M};L,V])+\alpha([\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime}])
and
\alpha([\underline{M};L,L])=0\leq Q
. This implies that if
[\underline{M};L,V]
is invertible, then
\alpha([\underline{M};L,V])=0
, that is,
\mu\big|_{V}=0
. Similarly, let
\beta(\underline{M};L,V)\leq\mathbb{Z}
denote the image of the homomorphism
V\otimes V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
corresponding to the bilinear function
\lambda\big|_{V\times V}:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\beta
also induces a well-defined function on
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, we have
\beta([\underline{M};L,V]\oplus[\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime}])=\beta([\underline{M};L,V])+\beta([\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime}])
and
\beta([\underline{M};L,L])=0\leq\mathbb{Z}
. So if
[\underline{M};L,V]
is invertible, then
\beta([\underline{M};L,V])=0
, that is,
\lambda\big|_{V\times V}=0
. This means that if
[\underline{M};L,V]
is invertible, then
V
is a lagrangian, therefore
[\underline{M};L,V]\in L_{2q+1}(Q,v)
.
∎

Proposition 4.27
.

We have
L_{2q+1}(0,0)\cong L_{2q+1}
, where
L_{2q+1}
denotes the classical L-group used in simply-connected surgery (in particular
L_{2q+1}\cong 0
).

Proof.

Kreck
[
20
, Section 6]
gives the following equivalent description of
L_{2q+1}
: an element of
L_{2q+1}
is represented by a pair
(\underline{H}_{2k},K)
for some
k\geq 0
, where
K
is a lagrangian in
\underline{H}_{2k}
. The equivalence relation on such pairs is generated by the elementary equivalences
(\underline{H}_{2k},K)\sim(\underline{H}_{2k}\oplus\underline{H}_{2},K\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}))
and
(\underline{H}_{2k},K)\sim(\underline{H}_{2k},\Phi(K))
for
\Phi\in RU(\underline{H}_{2k},\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
.

We define
F:L_{2q+1}\rightarrow L_{2q+1}(0,0)
by
F([\underline{H}_{2k},K])=[\underline{H}_{2k};\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k},K]
, this map is well-defined by Lemma
4.18
.

To define
G:L_{2q+1}(0,0)\rightarrow L_{2q+1}
, note that if
[\underline{H};L,K]\in L_{2q+1}(0,0)
, then
\underline{H}
is hyperbolic (see Lemma
2.13
), so by Lemma
2.23
there is a
k\geq 0
and an isomorphism
I:\underline{H}\rightarrow\underline{H}_{2k}
such that
I(L)=\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. Let
G([\underline{H};L,K])=[\underline{H}_{2k},I(K)]
, we will check that this is well-defined. If
I:\underline{H}\rightarrow\underline{H}_{2k}
and
I^{\prime}:\underline{H}\rightarrow\underline{H}_{2k}
are two isomorphisms as above, then
I^{\prime}\circ I^{-1}(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})=\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, so
I^{\prime}\circ I^{-1}\in RU(\underline{H}_{2k},\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
and
[\underline{H}_{2k},I(K)]=[\underline{H}_{2k},I^{\prime}(K)]
. If
(\underline{H};L,K)\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{H}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},K^{\prime})
, then
I:\underline{H}\rightarrow\underline{H}_{2k}
and
I^{\prime}:\underline{H}{}^{\prime}\rightarrow\underline{H}_{2l}
can be chosen such that one of
(\underline{H}_{2k},I(K))
and
(\underline{H}_{2l},I^{\prime}(K^{\prime}))
can be obtained from the other by stabilisation. Finally consider the equivalence
(\underline{H}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),K)\sim(\underline{H}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}),K)
. If
I:\underline{H}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H}_{2}\rightarrow\underline{H}_{2k}
is an isomorphism such that
I(L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}))=\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, then
I\circ(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{H^{\prime}}\oplus\sigma)(L\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}))=\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
I\circ(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{H^{\prime}}\oplus\sigma)\circ I^{-1}\in RU(\underline{H}_{2k},\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})
, therefore
[\underline{H}_{2k},I(K)]=[\underline{H}_{2k},I\circ(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{H^{\prime}}\oplus\sigma)(K)]
.

F
and
G
are obviously inverses of each other, and they preserve direct sums, therefore they are isomorphisms.
∎

Lemma 4.28
.

Suppose that
(\underline{M};L,V)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. If
\underline{H}
is a hyperbolic form and
K
and
J
are lagrangians in
\underline{H}
, then
(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{H};K,J)\sim(\underline{M};L,V)
.

Proof.

The quasi-formation
(\underline{H};K,J)
represents an element of
L_{2q+1}(0,0)
. By Proposition
4.27

L_{2q+1}(0,0)\cong 0
, so
(\underline{H};K,J)\sim(\underline{H};K,K)
in
\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(0,0)
. By comparing the equivalence relations that define
\ell_{2q+1}(0,0)
and
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, we see that
(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{H};K,J)\sim(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{H};K,K)\in\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. By applying Lemma
4.22
we get that
(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus(\underline{H};K,J)\sim(\underline{M};L,V)
.
∎

Theorem 4.29
.

If
Q
is free, then
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)\cong 0
.

Proof.

Let
[\underline{M};L,K]
be an arbitrary element in
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)
.

Let
J=K\cap L
. Since
K
and
L
are direct summands in
M
, the same holds for
J
(see Lemma
2.4
). Let
X
be a direct complement to
J
in
M
,
K^{\prime}=K\cap X
and
L^{\prime}=L\cap X
, then
K=J\oplus K^{\prime}
and
L=J\oplus L^{\prime}
.

Let
M^{\prime}=J\oplus X^{\perp}
. It follows from Lemma
2.7
that this is a subgroup of
M
and
\lambda\big|_{M^{\prime}\times M^{\prime}}
is nonsingular. Let
H=(M^{\prime})^{\perp}
, by Lemma
2.6

M=M^{\prime}\oplus H
. Let
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}=(M^{\prime},\lambda\big|_{M^{\prime}\times M^{\prime}},\mu\big|_{M^{\prime}})
and
\underline{H}=(H,\lambda\big|_{H\times H},\mu\big|_{H})
, then
\underline{M}=\underline{M}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H}
, and since
\underline{M}
is nonsingular,
\underline{H}
is nonsingular too.

Next we show that
J
is a lagrangian in
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
. Since
J
is a direct summand in
M
, it is also a direct summand in
M^{\prime}
. We have
\lambda\big|_{J\times J}=0
and
\mu\big|_{J}=0
, because
J\leq L
. Finally
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits M^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits J+\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits X^{\perp}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits J+(\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits M-\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits X)=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits J+\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits J=2\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits J
. So
J
is a lagrangian in
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
, and this means that
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
is metabolic.

Next we show that
L^{\prime}
is a lagrangian in
\underline{H}
. We have
L^{\prime}=L\cap X=L^{\perp}\cap X\leq J^{\perp}\cap X=(J\oplus X^{\perp})^{\perp}=H
. By construction
L^{\prime}
is a direct summand in
L
, hence in
M
, hence in
H
. We have
\lambda\big|_{L^{\prime}\times L^{\prime}}=0
and
\mu\big|_{L^{\prime}}=0
, because
L^{\prime}\leq L
. Finally
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits L^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits L-\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits J=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits M-\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits M^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits M-\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits M^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H
. Therefore
L^{\prime}
is a lagrangian in
\underline{H}
, and since
\underline{H}
is nonsingular, it is metabolic. We can show similarly that
K^{\prime}
is also a lagrangian in
\underline{H}
.

Consider the subgroup
K^{\prime}\oplus L^{\prime}\leq H
(note that
K^{\prime}\cap L^{\prime}=\{0\}
). Since
K^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime}
are lagrangians in
H
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits(K^{\prime}\oplus L^{\prime})=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H
, therefore
H/(K^{\prime}\oplus L^{\prime})
is a torsion group. The homomorphism
\mu\big|_{H}:H\rightarrow Q
induces a homomorphism
H/(K^{\prime}\oplus L^{\prime})\rightarrow Q
, because
\mu\big|_{K^{\prime}\oplus L^{\prime}}=0
. Since
Q
is assumed to be free, this induced homomorphism is trivial, therefore
\mu\big|_{H}=0
. This implies that
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
is full (because
\underline{M}
is full). Moreover, since
\underline{M}
is geometric (with respect to
v
), the same holds for
\underline{H}
, so
\underline{H}
is even. By applying Lemma
2.13
, we get that
\underline{H}
is hyperbolic.

Then we have
(\underline{M};L,K)=(\underline{M}{}^{\prime}\oplus\underline{H};J\oplus L^{\prime},J\oplus K^{\prime})=(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};J,J)\oplus(\underline{H};L^{\prime},K^{\prime})\sim(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};J,J)
by Lemma
4.28
. By Proposition
4.20
b)
(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};J,J)
represents the zero element in
\ell_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, and hence in
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)
. Therefore
[\underline{M};L,K]
is the zero element in
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)
, showing that
L_{2q+1}(Q,v)\cong 0
.
∎

4.4.
The
\ell
-monoid associated to a space

Definition 4.30
.

For a stable bundle
\xi
over a space
B
and an integer
q\geq 0
let
\hat{v}_{q}(\xi):H_{q}(B)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
denote the homomorphism determined by the Wu class
v_{q}(\xi)\in H^{q}(B;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
of
\xi
.

Definition 4.31
.

For a simply-connected space
B
, a stable bundle
\xi
over it and an even integer
q\geq 0
let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi))\text{\,,}

\displaystyle{g}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle={g}_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi))\text{\,,}

\displaystyle{s}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle={s}_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi))\text{\,,}

\displaystyle\ell_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle=\ell_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi))\text{\,,}

\displaystyle L_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle=L_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi))\text{\,,}

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi),\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q-1}(B))\text{\,,}

\displaystyle{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle={g}^{T}_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi),\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q-1}(B))\text{\,,}

\displaystyle{s}_{2q+1}^{T}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle={s}^{T}_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi),\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q-1}(B))\text{\,,}

\displaystyle\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle=\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi),\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q-1}(B))\text{\,,}

\displaystyle L_{2q+1}^{T}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle=L^{T}_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi),\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q-1}(B))\text{\,.}

5.
Normal maps and Q-forms

5.1.
Normal maps

We recall the terminology related to normal maps and normal smoothings, see
[
20
]
. Suppose that
B
is a space with a stable vector bundle
\xi
over it (equivalently, with a map
B\rightarrow BO
).

Definition 5.1
.

Let
M
be a manifold. A
normal map
over
(B,\xi)
is a pair
(f,\bar{f})
, where
f:M\rightarrow B
is a continuous map and
\bar{f}:\nu_{M}\rightarrow\xi
is a bundle map from the stable normal bundle
\nu_{M}
of
M
to
\xi
covering
f
. We will usually suppress
\bar{f}
from the notation and say that
f
is a normal map.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
M_{i}
be a closed manifold and
f_{i}:M_{i}\rightarrow B
be a normal map over
(B,\xi)
(
i=0,1
). A
normal bordism
between
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
is a normal map
F:Y\rightarrow B
such that
Y
is a cobordism between
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
, and
F\big|_{M_{i}}=f_{i}
(and
\bar{F}\big|_{\nu_{M_{i}}}=\bar{f}_{i}
).

If the manifolds
M_{i}
have boundaries and there is an identification
\partial M_{0}\approx\partial M_{1}
such that the normal maps
f_{i}\big|_{\partial M_{i}}
coincide, then
normal bordism rel boundary
can be defined analogously, with the additional condition that
F
restricts to the trivial normal bordism between the boundaries.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
j\geq 0
be an integer. A normal map
f:M\rightarrow B
over
(B,\xi)
is called a
normal
j
-smoothing
, if it is
(j+1)
-connected (ie.
\pi_{i}(f):\pi_{i}(M)\rightarrow\pi_{i}(B)
is an isomorphism for
i\leq j
and
\pi_{j+1}(f)
is surjective).

Definition 5.4
.

Let
j\geq 0
be an integer. We say that
(B,\xi)
is the
normal
j
-type
of a manifold
M
, if
M
admits a normal
j
-smoothing over
(B,\xi)
, and the classifying map
g:B\rightarrow BO
of
\xi
is
(j+1)
-co-connected (ie.
\pi_{i}(g):\pi_{i}(B^{j}(M))\rightarrow\pi_{i}(BO)
is an isomorphism for
i>j+1
and
\pi_{j+1}(g)
is injective).

Equivalently,
(B,\xi)
is the normal
j
-type of
M
, if
B
is the
(j+1)
-stage of the Moore-Postnikov tower of the classifying map
M\rightarrow BO
of
\nu_{M}
, and
\xi
is the pullback of the universal bundle to
B
. In particular, the normal
j
-type of
M
always exists, and it is unique up to homotopy equivalence that commutes with the maps
M\rightarrow B
and
B\rightarrow BO
(see Baues
[
1
, Section (5.3)]
). Consequently, if the normal
j
-type
(B,\xi)
is fixed, then any two normal
j
-smoothings
M\rightarrow B
differ by an automorphism of
(B,\xi)
.

5.2.
The Q-form of a map

Next we define the Q-form of a map, and prove some of its basic properties. Fix a topological space
B
and let
q\geq 2
be an even integer. Let
M
be a closed oriented
2q
-manifold with a map
f:M\rightarrow B
.

Definition 5.5
.

The
Q-form
of
f
is the extended quadratic form

E_{q}(M,f)=(H_{q}(M),\lambda,\mu)

over
H_{q}(B)
, where
\lambda:H_{q}(M)\times H_{q}(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is the intersection form of
M
and
\mu=H_{q}(f):H_{q}(M)\rightarrow H_{q}(B)
. (The bilinear function
\lambda
is symmetric, because
q
is even.)

It follows from Poincaré-duality that
E_{q}(M,f)
is nonsingular.

From the definitions we easily get the following lemmas:

Lemma 5.6
.

If
t:S^{q}\times S^{q}\rightarrow B
is nullhomotopic, then
E_{q}(S^{q}\times S^{q},t)\cong\underline{H}_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

If
N
is another closed oriented
2q
-manifold with a map
g:N\rightarrow B
, then
E_{q}(M\sqcup N,f\sqcup g)=E_{q}(M\#N,f\#g)=E_{q}(M,f)\oplus E_{q}(N,g)
.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

If
-M
denotes the same manifold
M
with opposite orientation, then
E_{q}(-M,f)=-E_{q}(M,f)^{*}
.

Proof.

Changing the orientation of
M
changes the sign of the intersection form
\lambda
, therefore
E_{q}(-M,f)=(H_{q}(M),-\lambda,\mu)=-E_{q}(M,f)^{*}
.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

If
f
is
q
-connected, then
E_{q}(M,f)
is full.
∎

Now let
\xi
be a stable bundle over
B
, and recall that
\hat{v}_{q}(\xi):H_{q}(B)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
denotes the homomorphism determined by the Wu class
v_{q}(\xi)\in H^{q}(B;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
of
\xi
.

Lemma 5.10
.

If
f^{*}(\xi)\cong\nu_{M}
, then
E_{q}(M,f)
is geometric with respect to
\hat{v}_{q}(\xi)
.

Proof.

By the assumption
f^{*}(v_{q}(\xi))=v_{q}(f^{*}(\xi))=v_{q}(\nu_{M})=v_{q}(M)\in H^{q}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

Let
x\in H_{q}(M)
be an arbitrary element, let
\alpha\in H^{q}(M)
be its Poincaré-dual, and let
\alpha_{2}\in H^{q}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
be the mod
2
reduction of
\alpha
. Since
\lambda(x,x)=\left<[M],\alpha\cup\alpha\right>=\left<[M],\alpha^{2}\right>
and
\alpha_{2}^{2}=Sq^{q}(\alpha_{2})=\alpha_{2}\cup v_{q}(M)
, we have

\varrho_{2}\circ\lambda(x,x)=\varrho_{2}(\left<[M],\alpha^{2}\right>)=\left<[M],\alpha_{2}^{2}\right>=\left<[M],\alpha_{2}\cup v_{q}(M)\right>=\left<x,v_{q}(M)\right>=\\
=\left<f_{*}(x),v_{q}(\xi)\right>=\left<\mu(x),v_{q}(\xi)\right>=\hat{v}_{q}(\xi)\circ\mu(x)\text{\,.}

Therefore
E_{q}(M,f)
is geometric with respect to
\hat{v}_{q}(\xi)
.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

Suppose that
M
is the boundary of some oriented manifold
W
and there is a map
F:W\rightarrow B
such that
F\big|_{M}=f
. If
X=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q+1}(W,M)\rightarrow H_{q}(M))
denotes the image of the boundary homomorphism, then

a)
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits X=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(M)
.

b)
\lambda\big|_{X\times X}=0
.

c)
\mu\big|_{X}=0
.

Proof.

a) and b) are well-known, see eg.
[
16
, §8]
.

c) Regarding
F
as a map of pairs
(W,M)\rightarrow(B,B)
, we get a morphism between the homological long exact sequences, and a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W,M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H_{q}(F)}
\textstyle{H_{q}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H_{q}(f)}
\textstyle{H_{q+1}(B,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(B)}

Since
H_{q+1}(B,B)\cong 0
, the composition
H_{q+1}(W,M)\rightarrow H_{q}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\rightarrow}}H_{q}(B)
is trivial.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

Suppose that
H_{q}(B)
is free. If
M
is the boundary of some oriented manifold
W
and there is a map
F:W\rightarrow B
such that
F\big|_{M}=f
, then
E_{q}(M,f)
is metabolic.

Proof.

Let
X=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q+1}(W,M)\rightarrow H_{q}(M))
, then
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits X=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(M)
,
\lambda\big|_{X\times X}=0
and
\mu\big|_{X}=0
by Lemma
5.11
.

Let
Y=\{y\in H_{q}(M)\mid ky\in X\text{ for some $k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}$}\}
. Then
Y
is a direct summand in
H_{q}(M)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q}(M)\leq Y
by Lemma
2.4
. Since
X\leq Y
and
Y/X
is a torsion group,
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits Y=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits X
. We have
\lambda\big|_{Y\times Y}=0
, because for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
there is
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
such that
k_{1}y_{1},k_{2}y_{2}\in X
, so
\lambda(k_{1}y_{1},k_{2}y_{2})=0
.

The homomorphism
\mu\big|_{Y}:Y\rightarrow H_{q}(B)
induces a homomorphism
Y/X\rightarrow H_{q}(B)
, because
\mu\big|_{X}=0
. Since
Y/X
is a torsion group and
H_{q}(B)
is free, this induced homomorphism is trivial, therefore
\mu\big|_{Y}=0
.

Therefore
Y
is a T-lagrangian in
E_{q}(M,f)
. Since
E_{q}(M,f)
is nonsingular, this means that it is metabolic.
∎

6.
The extended surgery obstruction
\theta_{W,F}

The following setting will be used throughout this section: Let
B
be a simply-connected space,
\xi
a stable vector bundle over
B
and
q\geq 2
an even integer. Let
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
be closed oriented
2q
-manifolds, and let
W
be an oriented cobordism between
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
, ie.
\partial W=M_{0}\sqcup(-M_{1})
. Let
f_{0}:M_{0}\rightarrow B
and
f_{1}:M_{1}\rightarrow B
be normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings over
(B,\xi)
(note that we suppress the bundle maps from the notation, see Section
5.1
). Let
F:W\rightarrow B
be a normal bordism between
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
, and assume that
F
is also a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing (ie. it is
q
-connected). Assume also that
\chi(M_{0})=\chi(M_{1})
(where
\chi(M_{i})
denotes the Euler-characteristic of
M_{i}
).

We will define an invariant
\theta_{W,F}\in{s}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
. This invariant, and its equivalence class
[\theta_{W,F}]\in\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
is the
extended surgery obstruction
associated to the normal bordism
F:W\rightarrow B
.

To define
\theta_{W,F}
we need to make two choices (and then we will show that
\theta_{W,F}
is independent of these choices). First, we choose a CW-decomposition
c
of
W
(ie. a homeomorphism between a CW-complex and
W
).
Let
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
denote the
q
-skeleton of
W
with respect to the CW-decomposition
c
. The inclusion
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W\rightarrow W
is isotopic to some embedding
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits W
into the interior of
W
. Our second choice is a closed regular neighbourhood
U\subset\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits W
of such an embedding.

After making these two choices, we will define a quasi-formation
\theta_{U,F}\in\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
. Then we define
\theta_{c,F}\in{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
to be the isomorphism class of
\theta_{U,F}
and we show that it only depends on the choice of
c
, but not on the choice of
U
. Next we define
\theta_{W,F}
to be the stable isomorphism class represented by
\theta_{c,F}
and show that it does not depend on the choice of
c
. Finally we consider
[\theta_{W,F}]
and show that it depends only on the normal bordism class (rel boundary) of
F:W\rightarrow B
.

To sum up, we will consider the following invariants:

\displaystyle\theta_{U,F}

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle\theta_{c,F}

\displaystyle\in{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle\theta_{W,F}

\displaystyle\in{s}_{2q+1}^{T}(B,\xi)

\displaystyle[\theta_{W,F}]

\displaystyle\in\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)

We will also prove two realisation results. First, any isomorphism class in the stable isomorphism class
\theta_{W,F}
can be obtained, after stabilisation with some
\mathcal{H}_{2k}
, as
\theta_{c,F}
for a CW-decomposition
c
of
W
(this will be a straightforward consequence of lemmas used earlier). Second, any stable isomorphism class in the equivalence class
[\theta_{W,F}]
is realised as
\theta_{W^{\prime},F^{\prime}}
for a normal bordism
F^{\prime}:W^{\prime}\rightarrow B
that is normally bordant (rel boundary) to
F
.

Finally we prove that
W
is an h-cobordism if and only if
\theta_{W,F}
is elementary, and
F
is normally bordant (rel boundary) to an
F^{\prime}:W^{\prime}\rightarrow B
with
W^{\prime}
an h-cobordism if and only if
[\theta_{W,F}]
is elementary, justifying the name “obstruction” for the invariants
\theta_{W,F}
and
[\theta_{W,F}]
.

6.1.
The definition of
\theta_{U,F}

Assume that a
c
and
U
are chosen as above.

Proposition 6.1
.

a) The spaces
W
,
M_{0}
,
M_{1}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
,
U
,
\partial U
and
W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U
are all simply-connected.

b) If
i\leq q-1
, then
H_{i}(B)\cong H_{i}(W)\cong H_{i}(M_{0})\cong H_{i}(M_{1})\cong H_{i}(\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W)\cong H_{i}(U)\cong H_{i}(\partial U)\cong H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U)
. The isomorphisms are induced by the natural inclusions between these spaces and (the restrictions of)
F
.

c) If
i\leq q
, then
H_{i}(W,U)\cong H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U)\cong 0
.

d) If
i\leq q
, then
H_{i}(W,W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U)\cong H_{i}(U,\partial U)\cong 0
.

e) If
i\leq q-1
, then
H_{i}(W,M_{0})\cong H_{i}(W,M_{1})\cong H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\cong H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})\cong 0
.

Proof.

Use the fact that the maps
F
,
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
, as well as the inclusion
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W\simeq U\rightarrow W
, are
q
-connected,
B
is simply-connected, and
q\geq 2
. Moreover, since
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
is a codimension-
(q+1)
subcomplex in
U
and
W
, the inclusions
\partial U\simeq U\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W\rightarrow U
and
W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U\simeq W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W\rightarrow W
are
q
-connected too. (For more details see
[
23
, Proposition 4.4.1]
.)
∎

Since
W
is oriented, the codimension-
0
submanifold
U
and its boundary
\partial U
are oriented too.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\underline{M}=E_{q}(\partial U,F\big|_{\partial U})
(see Definition
5.5
), that is

\underline{M}=(H_{q}(\partial U),\lambda,\mu)

where
\lambda:H_{q}(\partial U)\times H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is the intersection form of
\partial U
and
\mu=H_{q}(F\big|_{\partial U}):H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(B)
. This is an extended quadratic form over
H_{q}(B)
.

Lemma 6.3
.

\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q}(\partial U)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q-1}(B)
.

Proof.

Since
U\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
and
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
is
q
-dimensional,
H_{q}(U)
is free and
H_{q+1}(U)\cong 0
. Using the exact sequence of the pair
(U,\partial U)
, we get
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q}(\partial U)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)
. By the universal coefficient formula we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H^{q+2}(U,\partial U)
. By Poincaré-duality and Proposition
6.1
b)
H^{q+2}(U,\partial U)\cong H_{q-1}(U)\cong H_{q-1}(B)
.
∎

Definition 6.4
.

Let
L\leq H_{q}(\partial U)
denote the image of the boundary homomorphism
H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U)
.

Proposition 6.5
.

L
is a T-lagrangian in
\underline{M}
.

Proof.

Again, we have that
H_{q}(U)
is free, so
\mathop{\mathrm{Coker}}\nolimits(H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U))\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(U))\leq H_{q}(U)
is free too. By Lemma
2.4

L=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U))
is a direct summand in
H_{q}(\partial U)
, and it contains
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q}(\partial U)
. By applying Lemma
5.11
we also get
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits L=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(\partial U)
,
\lambda\big|_{L\times L}=0
and
\mu\big|_{L}=0
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

H_{q}(\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W)\cong H_{q}(\partial U)/L
, and the quotient homomorphism
H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U)/L\cong H_{q}(\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W)
is induced by the inclusion
\partial U\rightarrow U\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
.

Proof.

The homomorphism
H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(U)
is surjective, because
H_{q}(U,\partial U)\cong 0
(see Proposition
6.1
d)). Hence
H_{q}(\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W)\cong H_{q}(U)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(U))\cong H_{q}(\partial U)/\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(U))\cong H_{q}(\partial U)/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U))
.
∎

Consider the long exact sequence of the triple
(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0},M_{0})
:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 48.37552pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-48.37552pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.37552pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.37552pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 219.13129pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 219.13129pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\\
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U\sqcup M_{0},M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 139.32379pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 139.32379pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 249.9498pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 249.9498pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Definition 6.7
.

Let
V\leq H_{q}(\partial U)
denote the image of the boundary homomorphism
H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U\sqcup M_{0},M_{0})\cong H_{q}(\partial U)
.

Lemma 6.8
.

The homomorphism
H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})
is trivial.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
6.1
b)
that the boundary map
H_{q}(W,M_{0})\rightarrow H_{q-1}(M_{0})
is trivial.

By Proposition
6.1
c)
H_{i}(W,U)\cong 0
for
i\leq q
, so the boundary homomorphism
H_{q}(W,U\sqcup M_{0})\rightarrow H_{q-1}(U\sqcup M_{0},U)
in the exact sequence of the triple
(W,U\sqcup M_{0},U)
is an isomorphism. By excision we get the isomorphism in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{q}(W,M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{H_{q-1}(M_{0})}

showing that the map
H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})
is trivial.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

The homomorphism
H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\rightarrow H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})
is trivial.

Proof.

By Poincaré-duality it is enough to prove that the homomorphism
H^{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{1})\rightarrow H^{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})
is trivial.

Since
F
and
f_{1}
are
q
-connected, the inclusion
M_{1}\rightarrow W
induces an isomorphism on
H^{i}
for
i\leq q-1
, so the boundary map
H^{q-1}(M_{1})\rightarrow H^{q}(W,M_{1})
is trivial.

Since
U\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
, we have
H^{i}(W,U)\cong 0
for
i\leq q
, therefore the boundary homomorphism
H^{q-1}(U\sqcup M_{1},U)\rightarrow H^{q}(W,U\sqcup M_{1})
in the exact sequence of the triple
(W,U\sqcup M_{1},U)
is an isomorphism. By excision we get the isomorphism in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{q-1}(M_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{q}(W,M_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})}

showing that the map
H^{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{1})\rightarrow H^{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})
is trivial.
∎

By combining Lemmas
6.8
and
6.9
we get:

Theorem 6.10
.

There is a short exact sequence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 176.25577pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 176.25577pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 242.46857pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 242.46857pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 353.09457pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 353.09457pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\qed

Lemma 6.11
.

The restriction of the homomorphism
H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})
is an isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q}(\partial U)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})
.

Proof.

The universal coefficient formula, applied dually to compute the integral homology of a space
X
(with finitely generated cohomology groups) from its cohomology, gives a natural isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}\nolimits(H^{i+1}(X),\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{i}(X)
for every
i
.
Therefore it is enough to prove that the homomorphism
H^{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\rightarrow H^{q+1}(\partial U)
is an isomorphism.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{H^{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(W,U\sqcup M_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{q+1}(\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q-1}(\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q-1}(U)}

By Poincaré-duality the first square commutates and the horizontal arrows in it are isomorphisms. The second square also commutates, because it is induced by a map
(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{1},M_{1})\rightarrow(W,U\sqcup M_{1},M_{1})
of triples, and its horizontal arrows are isomorphisms by excision and Proposition
6.1
b).

It is now enough to prove that the map
H_{q}(W,U\sqcup M_{1})\rightarrow H_{q-1}(U)
is an isomorphism. This holds, because in the exact sequence of the triple
(W,U\sqcup M_{1},M_{1})
we have
H_{q-1}(W,M_{1})\cong 0
and the map
H_{q}(W,M_{1})\rightarrow H_{q}(W,U\sqcup M_{1})\cong H_{q-1}(M_{1})
is trivial by Proposition
6.1
e) and b) respectively (where we used the isomorphism from the proof of Lemma
6.8
).
∎

Lemma 6.12
.

We have
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})
.

Proof.

It follows from Poincaré-duality and the universal coefficient formula that
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H^{2q+1-i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{2q+1-i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})
for every
i
, in particular
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})
.

Since
\chi(M_{0})=\chi(M_{1})
, we have
\chi(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})=\chi(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})
, that is,
\sum_{i=0}^{2q+1}(-1)^{i}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})=\sum_{i=0}^{2q+1}(-1)^{i}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})
. Hence

\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})-\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})=\\
=\sum_{i\neq q}(-1)^{i+q+1}(\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})-\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1}))

If
i\leq q-1
, then
H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})\cong H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})\cong 0
by Proposition
6.1
e).

If
i\geq q+1
, then
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{2q+1-i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{2q-i}(M_{0})=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{2q-i}(B)
(the second equality follows from the proof of Lemma
6.8
and the third from Proposition
6.1
b)). Similarly
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{2q-i}(B)
, therefore
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})-\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{i}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})=0
.

It follows that
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{0})-\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits H_{q}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,M_{1})=0
.
∎

Definition 6.13
.

Let
\theta_{U,F}
denote the triple
(\underline{M};L,V)
(see Definitions
6.2
,
6.4
and
6.7
).

Proposition 6.14
.

\theta_{U,F}
is a quasi-formation over
H_{q}(B)
and
\theta_{U,F}\in\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
.

Proof.

\underline{M}=E_{q}(\partial U,F\big|_{\partial U})
is an extended quadratic form over
H_{q}(B)
. It is metabolic, because the intersection form
\lambda
is nonsingular, and
L
is a T-lagrangian (Proposition
6.5
). It follows from Theorem
6.10
and Lemmas
6.11
and
6.12
that
V
is a free half-rank direct summand. Therefore
\theta_{U,F}
is a quasi-formation over
H_{q}(B)
.

Since
F
is a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing,
F^{*}(\xi)\cong\nu_{W}
and hence
(F\big|_{\partial U})^{*}(\xi)\cong\nu_{\partial U}
. By Lemma
5.10
this implies that
\underline{M}
is geometric with respect to
\hat{v}_{q}(\xi)
. It is also full, because
F\big|_{\partial U}
is the composition
\partial U\rightarrow U\rightarrow W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F}}{{\rightarrow}}B
of three
q
-connected maps (using Proposition
6.1
d), the homotopy equivalence
U\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
and the assumption on
F
). By Lemma
6.3

\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q}(\partial U)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q-1}(B)
. Therefore
\theta_{U,F}
is in the class
\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)=\mathfrak{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(H_{q}(B),\hat{v}_{q}(\xi),\mathop{\mathrm{Tor}}\nolimits H_{q-1}(B))
.
∎

6.2.
The definition of
\theta_{c,F}

Now suppose that only a CW-decomposition
c
of
W
is chosen, without a fixed regular neighbourhood
U
of
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
.

Definition 6.15
.

Let
\theta_{c,F}\in{g}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
denote the isomorphism class of
\theta_{U,F}
for any regular neighbourhood
U
of any embedding
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits W
(isotopic to the inclusion
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W\rightarrow W
).

Proposition 6.16
.

\theta_{c,F}
is well-defined.

Proof.

Let
U
and
U^{\prime}
be regular neighbourhoods of two embeddings
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits W
(isotopic to the inclusion
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W\rightarrow W
), and let
(\underline{M};L,V)=\theta_{U,F}
and
(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})=\theta_{U^{\prime},F}
, then we need to show that
(\underline{M};L,V)\cong(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})
. By Theorem
6.17
b) there is a diffeomorphism
g:U\rightarrow U^{\prime}
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{U}
and
g
are isotopic as
U\rightarrow W
embeddings. It follows from the isotopy extension theorem that there is a diffeomorphism
G:W\rightarrow W
(isotopic to the identity) such that
G\big|_{U}=g
, hence
G(U)=U^{\prime}
. Then
H_{q}(G\big|_{\partial U}):H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})
is an isomorphism between
\underline{M}
and
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
G
induces commutative diagrams

\textstyle{H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{q+1}(U^{\prime},\partial U^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})}

\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U^{\prime},\partial U^{\prime}\sqcup M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})}

Hence
H_{q}(G\big|_{\partial U})
sends
L
to
L^{\prime}
and
V
to
V^{\prime}
. Therefore
(\underline{M};L,V)
and
(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})
are isomorphic.
∎

We used the following:

Theorem 6.17

(Wall
[
28
]
and Hudson
[
17
]
)
.

a) Suppose that
K
is a simply-connected finite CW-complex of dimension
k
, and
M
is a simply-connected
d
-manifold for some
d\geq\max(2k+1,k+3)
. Let
f:K\rightarrow M
be a continuous map. Then there is a compact codimension-
0
submanifold
N\subset M
with simply-connected boundary and a homotopy equivalence
h:K\rightarrow N
such that the composition
K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\rightarrow}}N\rightarrow M
is homotopic to
f
.

b) If
h:K\rightarrow N
and
h^{\prime}:K\rightarrow N^{\prime}
are two homotopy equivalences as above, then there is a diffeomorphism
g:N\rightarrow N^{\prime}
such that
g\circ h\simeq h^{\prime}:K\rightarrow N^{\prime}
and
N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}N^{\prime}\rightarrow M
is isotopic to the embedding
N\rightarrow M
.

Proof.

Part a) is a special case of Wall’s embedding theorem
[
28
]
. Part b) follows from the uniqueness part of the embedding theorem combined with results of Hudson
[
17
, Theorem 2.1 and Addendum 2.1.2]
.
∎

6.3.
The definition of
\theta_{W,F}

Now we will consider
W
without a fixed CW-decomposition
c
.

Definition 6.18
.

Let
\theta_{W,F}\in{s}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
be the stable isomorphism class of
\theta_{c,F}
for any CW-decomposition
c
of
W
.

Theorem 6.19
.

\theta_{W,F}
is a well-defined.

Proof.

Let
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
be arbitrary CW-decompositions of
W
. Then they have stabilisations (see Definition
6.21
)
c_{1}^{\prime}
and
c_{2}^{\prime}
that satisfy the conditions of Lemma
6.23
. By Lemmas
6.22
and
6.20
we have
\theta_{c_{1},F}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}\theta_{c_{1}^{\prime},F}=\theta_{c_{2}^{\prime},F}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}\theta_{c_{2},F}
. Therefore the stable isomorphism class of
\theta_{c,F}
is independent of the choice of
c
.
∎

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
be two CW-decompositions of
W
, and let
S_{i}\subset W
denote the
q
-skeleton of
W
with respect to
c_{i}
, for
i=1,2
. If there is a homotopy equivalence
h:S_{1}\rightarrow S_{2}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W}

commutes up to homotopy, then
\theta_{c_{1},F}=\theta_{c_{2},F}
.

Proof.

Let
U_{i}
be a regular neighbourhood of the embedding
S_{i}\rightarrow W
, so that
\theta_{c_{i},F}
is the isomorphism class of
\theta_{U_{i},F}
. Since
h
is a homotopy equivalence, the composition
S_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\rightarrow}}S_{2}\rightarrow U_{2}
is a homotopy equivalence too. Moreover, the commutativity of the diagram implies that the inclusion
S_{1}\rightarrow W
is homotopic to the composition
S_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\rightarrow}}S_{2}\rightarrow U_{2}\rightarrow W
. Therefore we can apply Theorem
6.17
b) to the map
S_{1}\rightarrow W
and the submanifolds
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
. It follows that
\theta_{U_{1},F}\cong\theta_{U_{2},F}
as in the proof of Proposition
6.16
. Therefore
\theta_{c_{1},F}=\theta_{c_{2},F}
.
∎

Definition 6.21
.

We define stabilisation, an operation on CW-decompositions of
W
, as follows: Let
c
be a CW-decomposition of
W
. Let
x
be a
0
-cell, since
W
is a
(2q+1)
-manifold,
x
is in the boundary of some
(2q+1)
-cell
e^{2q+1}
. Consider
D^{q+1}
with its decomposition into
3
cells (of dimensions
0
,
q
and
q+1
), and an embedding of
D^{q+1}
into
x\cup\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits e^{2q+1}
such that the
0
-cell goes to
x
. The new CW-decomposition
c^{\prime}
is constructed from
c
by adding the
q
-cell and
(q+1)
-cell of the embedded
D^{q+1}
and modifying the gluing map of the
(2q+1)
-cell
e^{2q+1}
.

Note that the stabilisation operation is not unique.

We say that
c^{\prime}
is a (
k
-fold) stabilisation of
c
, if it can be obtained from
c
by a sequence of (
k
) stabilisation steps.

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
c
and
c^{\prime}
be CW-decompositions of
W
. If
c^{\prime}
is a
k
-fold stabilisation of
c
, then
\theta_{c^{\prime},F}\cong\theta_{c,F}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2k}
. In particular,
\theta_{c^{\prime},F}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}\theta_{c,F}
for any stabilisation
c^{\prime}
of
c
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove this when
c^{\prime}
is obtained from
c
by a single stabilisation step.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
denote the
q
-skeletons of
W
with respect to
c
and
c^{\prime}
, then
S^{\prime}=S\vee S^{q}
. Let
U
and
U^{\prime}
be regular neighbourhoods of
S
and
S^{\prime}
, we can choose these such that
U^{\prime}\approx U\natural(S^{q}\times D^{q+1})
. Let
(\underline{M};L,V)=\theta_{U,F}
and
(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})=\theta_{U^{\prime},F}
.

Since
\partial U^{\prime}\approx U\#(S^{q}\times S^{q})
, we have
H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})\cong H_{q}(\partial U)\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}\cong\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2}
, we will use these canonical isomorphisms to identify these groups and extended quadratic forms. The inclusion
H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})
under this identification is in fact the composition
H_{q}(\partial U)\cong H_{q}(\partial U\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits D^{2q})\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})
, where the homomorphism
H_{q}(\partial U\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits D^{2q})\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})
is induced by the inclusion of spaces. Thus the inclusion
U\rightarrow U^{\prime}
induces commutative diagrams

\textstyle{H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q+1}(U^{\prime},\partial U^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})}

and

\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W,U\sqcup M_{0})\cong H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W,U^{\prime}\sqcup M_{0})\cong H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U^{\prime},\partial U^{\prime}\sqcup M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})}

hence
L\leq L^{\prime}
and
V\leq V^{\prime}
.

If we fix some basepoint
x\in S^{q}
, the submanifold
\{x\}\times D^{q+1}\subset S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\subseteq U^{\prime}
represents an element of
H_{q+1}(U^{\prime},\partial U^{\prime})
, and its boundary is represented by the submanifold
\{x\}\times S^{q}\subset S^{q}\times S^{q}\subseteq\partial U^{\prime}
. This shows that
(0,(0,1))\in L^{\prime}\leq H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime})\cong H_{q}(\partial U)\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, therefore
L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z})\leq L^{\prime}
. Since
L^{\prime}
and
L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z})
are both T-lagrangians in
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
, this implies that
L^{\prime}=L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z})
.

Similarly, the embedded
D^{q+1}
that we used to construct the stabilisation
c^{\prime}
from
c
represents an element of
H_{q+1}(W,U^{\prime}\sqcup M_{0})\cong H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U^{\prime},\partial U^{\prime}\sqcup M_{0})
, and its boundary is represented by
S^{q}\times\{x\}\subset S^{q}\times S^{q}\subseteq\partial U^{\prime}
, therefore
V\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\})\leq V^{\prime}
. Since
V^{\prime}
and
V\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\})
are both free half-rank direct summands in
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}
, this implies that
V^{\prime}=V\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\})
.

Therefore
(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})\cong(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),V\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}))=(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Since
\theta_{c,F}
and
\theta_{c^{\prime},F}
are the isomorphism classes of
\theta_{U,F}
and
\theta_{U^{\prime},F}
respectively, this means that
\theta_{c^{\prime},F}\cong\theta_{c,F}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
be any CW-decompositions of
W
. Then there is a stabilisation
c_{i}^{\prime}
of
c_{i}
(
i=1,2
) such that if
S_{i}^{\prime}\subset W
denotes the
q
-skeleton of
W
with respect to
c_{i}^{\prime}
, then there is a homotopy equivalence
h:S_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow S_{2}^{\prime}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{S_{1}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{S_{2}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W}

commutes up to homotopy.

Proof.

Let
S_{i}
denote the
q
-skeleton of
W
with respect to
c_{i}
and let
g_{i}:S_{i}\rightarrow W
denote the inclusion. Then the homomorphisms
H_{q}(g_{i}):H_{q}(S_{i})\rightarrow H_{q}(W)
are surjective, so by Lemma
2.21
a) there are free abelian groups
A_{i}
and an isomorphism
H:H_{q}(S_{1})\oplus A_{1}\rightarrow H_{q}(S_{2})\oplus A_{2}
such that
(H_{q}(g_{1})+0)=(H_{q}(g_{2})+0)\circ H:H_{q}(S_{1})\oplus A_{1}\rightarrow H_{q}(W)
.

Let
k_{i}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A_{i}
and let
c_{i}^{\prime}
be a
k_{i}
-fold stabilisation of
c_{i}
. Then
S_{i}^{\prime}\approx S_{i}\vee\bigvee^{k_{i}}S^{q}
, so
H_{q}(S_{i}^{\prime})\cong H_{q}(S_{i})\oplus A_{i}
, and
H
determines an isomorphism
H^{\prime}:H_{q}(S_{1}^{\prime})\rightarrow H_{q}(S_{2}^{\prime})
such that
H_{q}(g_{1}^{\prime})=H_{q}(g_{2}^{\prime})\circ H^{\prime}:H_{q}(S_{1}^{\prime})\rightarrow H_{q}(W)
, where
g_{i}^{\prime}:S_{i}^{\prime}\rightarrow W
is the inclusion.

By Lemma
6.24
there is a map
h:S_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow S_{2}^{\prime}
such that
g_{1}^{\prime}\simeq g_{2}^{\prime}\circ h:S_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow W
and
H_{q}(h)=H^{\prime}
. Since
H_{j}(g_{1}^{\prime})=H_{j}(g_{2}^{\prime})\circ H_{j}(h)
and
H_{j}(g_{1}^{\prime})
and
H_{j}(g_{2}^{\prime})
are isomorphisms if
j\leq q-1
, the same is true for
H_{j}(h)
. As
H_{q}(h)=H^{\prime}
is also an isomorphism, and
S_{1}^{\prime}
and
S_{2}^{\prime}
are simply-connected and
q
-dimensional,
h
is a homotopy equivalence.
∎

Lemma 6.24
.

Let
X
be a simply-connected topological space and
R
and
S
be CW-complexes with maps
f:R\rightarrow X
and
g:S\rightarrow X
. Assume that
R
is
q
-dimensional,
g
is
q
-connected and
J:H_{q}(R)\rightarrow H_{q}(S)
is a homomorphism such that
H_{q}(g)\circ J=H_{q}(f)
. Then there is a map
j:R\rightarrow S
such that
g\circ j\simeq f
and
H_{q}(j)=J
.

Proof.

Since
g
is
q
-connected and
R
is
q
-dimensional,
f
has a lift
\tilde{f}:R\rightarrow S
such that
g\circ\tilde{f}\simeq f
. Let
J_{0}=J-H_{q}(\tilde{f}):H_{q}(R)\rightarrow H_{q}(S)
.

The pair
(X,S)
is
q
-connected (where
S
is regarded as a subspace of
X
via
g
), so
\pi_{q+1}(X,S)\cong H_{q+1}(X,S)
. The Hurewicz-homomorphisms induce the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{H_{q}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{0}}
\textstyle{H_{q+1}(X,S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H_{q}(g)}
\textstyle{H_{q}(X)}
\textstyle{\pi_{q+1}(X,S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\pi_{q}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{q}(g)}
\textstyle{\pi_{q}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We have
H_{q}(g)\circ J_{0}=H_{q}(g)\circ(J-H_{q}(\tilde{f}))=H_{q}(g)\circ J-H_{q}(g)\circ H_{q}(\tilde{f})=H_{q}(f)-H_{q}(f)=0
and
H_{q}(R)
is free, so
J_{0}
can be lifted to a homomorphism
H_{q}(R)\rightarrow H_{q+1}(X,S)
. By composing this homomorphism with the inverse of the isomorphism
\pi_{q+1}(X,S)\rightarrow H_{q+1}(X,S)
and the boundary map, we get a homomorphism
\hat{J}_{0}:H_{q}(R)\rightarrow\pi_{q}(S)
such that
h\circ\hat{J}_{0}=J_{0}
. Moreover, it follows from the construction of
\hat{J}_{0}
that
\pi_{q}(g)\circ\hat{J}_{0}=0
, that is
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\hat{J}_{0})\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\pi_{q}(g))
.

Let
(C_{*}(R),\partial_{*})
be the cellular chain complex of
R
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\partial_{q})\cong C_{q}(R)/\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\partial_{q})
is a subgroup of
C_{q-1}(R)
, it is free, hence
H_{q}(R)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\partial_{q})
is a direct summand in
C_{q}(R)
. Therefore
\hat{J}_{0}:H_{q}(R)\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\pi_{q}(g))
can be extended to a homomorphism
\bar{J}_{0}:C_{q}(R)\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\pi_{q}(g))\leq\pi_{q}(S)
.

Given a
q
-cell
e^{q}
of
R
and an element
[a]\in\pi_{q}(S)
, we can modify the map
\tilde{f}
by adding
[a]
to
\tilde{f}\big|_{e^{q}}
, ie. by replacing
\tilde{f}\big|_{e^{q}}
with the composition
e^{q}\rightarrow e^{q}\vee S^{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{f}\vee a}}{{\longrightarrow}}S
. This operation changes the homomorphism
H_{q}(\tilde{f}):H_{q}(R,\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q-1}R)\rightarrow H_{q}(S,\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q-1}S)
by adding the image of
h([a])\in H_{q}(S)
in
H_{q}(S,\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q-1}S)
to the image of the generator
[e^{q}]\in H_{q}(R,\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q-1}R)\cong C_{q}(R)
. Moreover, if
[a]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\pi_{q}(g))
, then the homotopy class of
g\circ\tilde{f}
does not change, because
g\circ a
is nullhomotopic, so the composition
e^{q}\rightarrow e^{q}\vee S^{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{f}\vee a}}{{\longrightarrow}}S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}X
is homotopic to
g\circ\tilde{f}\big|_{e^{q}}
(rel boundary).

Let
j:R\rightarrow S
be the map obtained from
\tilde{f}
by adding
\bar{J}_{0}([e^{q}_{i}])\in\pi_{q}(S)
to
\tilde{f}\big|_{e^{q}_{i}}
for every
q
-cell
e^{q}_{i}
of
R
. This changes
H_{q}(\tilde{f}):H_{q}(R,\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q-1}R)\cong C_{q}(R)\rightarrow H_{q}(S,\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q-1}S)
by adding the composition
C_{q}(R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{J}_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}\pi_{q}(S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\rightarrow}}H_{q}(S)\rightarrow H_{q}(S,\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q-1}S)
. Therefore
H_{q}(j)=H_{q}(\tilde{f})+h\circ\bar{J}_{0}\big|_{H_{q}(R)}:H_{q}(R)\rightarrow H_{q}(S)
. Hence

H_{q}(j)=H_{q}(\tilde{f})+h\circ\bar{J}_{0}\big|_{H_{q}(R)}=H_{q}(\tilde{f})+h\circ\hat{J}_{0}=H_{q}(\tilde{f})+J_{0}=H_{q}(\tilde{f})+J-H_{q}(\tilde{f})=J\text{\,.}

Moreover, since
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(\bar{J}_{0})\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}\nolimits(\pi_{q}(g))
, we have
g\circ j\simeq g\circ\tilde{f}\simeq f
, therefore
j
satisfies both conditions.
∎

Proposition 6.25
.

If
\theta_{W,F}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{M};L,V)
for some
\underline{M}
,
L
and
V
, then there is a CW-decomposition
c
of
W
and an integer
k\geq 0
such that
\theta_{c,F}\cong(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2k}
.

Proof.

Let
c_{0}
be an arbitrary CW-decomposition of
W
. Then
\theta_{W,F}
is the stable isomorphism class of
\theta_{c_{0},F}
, so
\theta_{W,F}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{M};L,V)
means that there are integers
k,l\geq 0
such that
(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2k}\cong\theta_{c_{0},F}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2l}
. Let
c
be an
l
-fold stabilisation of
c_{0}
. Then by Lemma
6.22

\theta_{c,F}\cong\theta_{c_{0},F}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2l}\cong(\underline{M};L,V)\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2k}
.
∎

6.4.
The invariance of
[\theta_{W,F}]

Now we consider the equivalence class
[\theta_{W,F}]\in\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
of
\theta_{W,F}
and show that it is a normal bordism invariant.

Theorem 6.26
.

Suppose that
F^{\prime}:W^{\prime}\rightarrow B
is another
q
-connected normal bordism between
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
over
(B,\xi)
. If
F^{\prime}
is normally bordant to
F
(rel boundary), then
[\theta_{W,F}]=[\theta_{W^{\prime},F^{\prime}}]\in\ell^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
.

Proof.

Let
G:X\rightarrow B
be a normal bordism between
F
and
F^{\prime}
. By applying surgery below the middle dimension (see eg.
[
20
, Proposition 4]
), we can assume that
G
is
(q+1)
-connected. Since the compositions
W\rightarrow X\rightarrow B
and
W^{\prime}\rightarrow X\rightarrow B
are
q
-connected, this implies that the inclusions
W\rightarrow X
and
W^{\prime}\rightarrow X
are
q
-connected too. Then there is a Morse-function on the triad
(X,W,W^{\prime})
having only critical points of index
q+1
(see
[
21
, Theorem 8.1 and the proof of Theorem 7.8]
). This means that
W^{\prime}
can be obtained from
W
by a sequence of
q
-surgeries over
(B,\xi)
, so it is enough to prove the statement when
W^{\prime}
is obtained by a single
q
-surgery.

Fix an embedding
S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\rightarrow W
where the surgery is done, and let
W_{0}=W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits(S^{q}\times D^{q+1})
. Then
W^{\prime}=W_{0}\cup_{S^{q}\times S^{q}}D^{q+1}\times S^{q}
. Take the standard CW-decomposition of
S^{q}\times S^{q}
into
4
cells (coming from the product structure) and extend it to a CW-decomposition
c_{0}
of
W_{0}
. Define the equivalence relation
\underset{W}{\sim}
on
S^{q}\times S^{q}
by
(x,y_{1})\underset{W}{\sim}(x,y_{2})
for any
x,y_{1},y_{2}\in S^{q}
, then
{W_{0}}/{\underset{W}{\sim}}
is diffeomorphic to
W
. Moreover,
c_{0}
induces a CW-decomposition
c
on
{W_{0}}/{\underset{W}{\sim}}\approx W
, namely we have the standard decomposition of
{S^{q}\times S^{q}}/{\underset{W}{\sim}}\approx S^{q}
into
2
cells, and all other cells remain the same, with adjusted gluing maps.
If we fix these CW-decompositions, then
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
is of the form
S_{0}\vee S^{q}
, where
S_{0}
is the
q
-skeleton of
W_{0}
without the two
q
-cells of
S^{q}\times S^{q}
. Then a regular neighbourhood
U
of
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W
is of the form
U_{0}\natural(S^{q}\times D^{q+1})
, where
U_{0}
is a regular neighbourhood of
S_{0}
and we can assume that the embedding
S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\rightarrow U\rightarrow W
coincides with the embedding previously fixed for surgery (and hence
F\big|_{S^{q}\times D^{q+1}}
is nullhomotopic). This means that
\partial U\approx\partial U_{0}\#(S^{q}\times S^{q})
and
F\big|_{S^{q}\times S^{q}}
is nullhomotopic. Let
(\underline{M};L,V)=\theta_{U,F}
. By Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
we have
\underline{M}\cong\underline{M}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2}
, where
\underline{M}_{0}=(H_{q}(\partial U_{0}),\lambda_{0},\mu_{0})
,
\lambda_{0}
is the intersection form of
\partial U_{0}
and
\mu_{0}=H_{q}(F\big|_{\partial U_{0}})
. Moreover
L\cong L_{0}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z})
, where
L_{0}=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q+1}(U_{0},\partial U_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U_{0}))
.

Similarly we define the equivalence relation
\underset{W^{\prime}}{\sim}
on
S^{q}\times S^{q}
by
(x_{1},y)\underset{W^{\prime}}{\sim}(x_{2},y)
for any
x_{1},x_{2},y\in S^{q}
, then
{W_{0}}/{\underset{W^{\prime}}{\sim}}\approx W^{\prime}
. We get a CW-decomposition
c^{\prime}
of
W^{\prime}
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W^{\prime}\approx S_{0}\vee S^{q}
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{sk}}\nolimits_{q}W^{\prime}
has a regular neighbourhood
U^{\prime}\approx U_{0}\natural(D^{q+1}\times S^{q})
, where
U_{0}
is the same regular neighbourhood of
S_{0}
that we used earlier. We can assume that the image of the embedding
D^{q+1}\times S^{q}\rightarrow U^{\prime}\rightarrow W^{\prime}
is
\overline{W^{\prime}\setminus W_{0}}
, and the restriction of this embedding to
S^{q}\times S^{q}
coincides with the previously fixed embedding
S^{q}\times S^{q}\rightarrow W_{0}
. Let
(\underline{M}{}^{\prime};L^{\prime},V^{\prime})=\theta_{U^{\prime},F^{\prime}}
. We have
\partial U^{\prime}=\partial U\approx\partial U_{0}\#(S^{q}\times S^{q})
, therefore
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}=\underline{M}\cong\underline{M}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2}
and
L^{\prime}\cong L_{0}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\})
. Moreover,
W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U=W_{0}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U_{0}=W^{\prime}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U^{\prime}
, so
V=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q+1}(W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U,\partial U\sqcup M_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U))=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q+1}(W^{\prime}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits U^{\prime},\partial U^{\prime}\sqcup M_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime}))=V^{\prime}
. By the definition of the equivalence relation
\sim
(see Definition
4.10
), we have
\theta_{U,F}\cong(\underline{M}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L_{0}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),V)\sim(\underline{M}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L_{0}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}),V)\cong\theta_{U^{\prime},F^{\prime}}
. Therefore
\theta_{W,F}\sim\theta_{W^{\prime},F^{\prime}}
.
∎

6.5.
Realising stable isomorphism classes

Next we show that every stable isomorphism class in the equivalence class
[\theta_{W,F}]
can be realised by a normal bordism.

Theorem 6.27
.

If
\theta_{W,F}\sim x
for some
x\in{s}^{T}_{2q+1}(B,\xi)
, then there is a
q
-connected normal bordism
F^{\prime}:W^{\prime}\rightarrow B
that is normally bordant to
F
(rel boundary) such that
\theta_{W^{\prime},F^{\prime}}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}x
.

Proof.

If
\theta_{W,F}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),V)
and
x\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}),V)
for some
\underline{M}
,
L
and
V
, then we can apply Lemma
6.28
below to get a suitable
F^{\prime}
.

The case
\theta_{W,F}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}),V)
,
x\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),V)
can be reduced to the previous one by applying the automorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{M}\oplus\sigma
of
\underline{M}\oplus\underline{H}_{2}
(where
\sigma:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is the flip map).

In general,
\theta_{W,F}
is related to
x
by a sequence of the above elementary equivalences, so we can get an
F^{\prime}:W^{\prime}\rightarrow B
by a repeated application of Lemma
6.28
.
∎

Lemma 6.28
.

Suppose that
\theta_{W,F}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{M}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L_{0}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),V)
for some
\underline{M}_{0}
,
L_{0}
and
V
. Then there is a
q
-connected normal bordism
F^{\prime\prime}:W^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow B
which is normally bordant to
F
such that
\theta_{W^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime}}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}(\underline{M}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L_{0}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}),V)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.25
there is a CW-decomposition
c
of
W
and an integer
k\geq 0
such that
\theta_{c,F}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2k}\oplus(\underline{M}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L_{0}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),V)
. To simplify notation let
\underline{\bar{M}}_{0}=\underline{H}_{2k}\oplus\underline{M}_{0}
,
\bar{L}_{0}=(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})\oplus L_{0}
and
\bar{V}=(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\{0\})\oplus V
. Let
\bar{M}_{0}
denote the underlying group of
\underline{\bar{M}}_{0}
. Let
S
denote the
q
-skeleton of
W
with respect to
c
and let
U
be a regular neighbourhood of
S
, then by definition
\theta_{c,F}
is the isomorphism class of
\theta_{U,F}
. We fix an isomorphism between
\theta_{U,F}
and
(\underline{\bar{M}}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};\bar{L}_{0}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),\bar{V})
.

Let
\alpha\in H_{q}(W)
be the image of
(0,(1,0))\in\bar{M}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
under the homomorphism
H_{q}(\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q}(W)
induced by the inclusion. Since
(0,(1,0))
is in the hyperbolic component of
H_{q}(\partial U)
, we have
H_{q}(F\big|_{\partial U})(0,(1,0))=0
and hence
H_{q}(F)(\alpha)=0
.

Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H_{q+1}(B,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H_{q}(F)}
\textstyle{H_{q}(B)}
\textstyle{\pi_{q+1}(B,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\pi_{q}(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{q}(F)}
\textstyle{\pi_{q}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

induced by the Hurewicz-homomorphisms (where we regard
W
as a subspace of
B
via
F
). The pair
(B,W)
is
q
-connected, hence
\pi_{q+1}(B,W)\cong H_{q+1}(B,W)
.

Since
H_{q}(F)(\alpha)=0
,
\alpha
is the image of some element in
H_{q+1}(B,W)
. The image of the corresponding relative homotopy class in
\pi_{q+1}(B,W)
under the boundary homomorphism is an element
\hat{\alpha}\in\pi_{q}(W)
, which is mapped to
\alpha
by the Hurewicz-homomorphism, and it follows from the exactness of the sequence that
\pi_{q}(F)(\hat{\alpha})=0
.

Since
q<\frac{1}{2}\dim W
,
\hat{\alpha}
can be represented by an embedding
S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\rightarrow W
along which we can perform a surgery over
(B,\xi)
(see
[
20
, Lemma 2]
). Let
F^{\prime\prime}:W^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow B
be the result of this surgery, it is a normal bordism between
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
which is normally bordant to
F
by construction. Since
F
is
q
-connected and we did surgery along a
q
-sphere,
F^{\prime\prime}
is
q
-connected too (
[
20
, Lemma 1]
). We construct CW-decompositions
c^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
of
W
and
W^{\prime\prime}
respectively from a CW-decomposition of
W\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{int}}\nolimits(S^{q}\times D^{q+1})
as in the proof of Theorem
6.26
.

Let
S^{\prime}
denote the
q
-skeleton of
W
with respect to
c^{\prime}
, then
S^{\prime}\approx S^{\prime}_{0}\vee S^{q}
for some
S^{\prime}_{0}
, so
H_{q}(S^{\prime})\cong H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{Z}
. Since
S^{q}\subseteq S^{\prime}
represents the sphere along which we do surgery, the inclusion
S^{\prime}\rightarrow W
induces a homomorphism that sends
(0,1)\in H_{q}(S^{\prime})\cong H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{Z}
to
\alpha\in H_{q}(W)
. Let
U^{\prime}
be a regular neighbourhood of
S^{\prime}
, then
U^{\prime}
is of the form
U^{\prime}_{0}\natural(S^{q}\times D^{q+1})
, and we can assume that the composition
S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\rightarrow U^{\prime}\rightarrow W
coincides with the embedding used for the surgery. Therefore
\theta_{U^{\prime},F}\cong(\underline{M}{}^{\prime}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L^{\prime}_{0}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),V^{\prime})
, where
\underline{M}{}^{\prime}_{0}=(H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime}_{0}),\lambda^{\prime}_{0},\mu^{\prime}_{0})
,
\lambda^{\prime}_{0}
is the intersection form of
\partial U^{\prime}_{0}
,
\mu^{\prime}_{0}=H_{q}(F\big|_{\partial U^{\prime}_{0}})
and
L^{\prime}_{0}=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(H_{q+1}(U^{\prime}_{0},\partial U^{\prime}_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(\partial U^{\prime}_{0}))
. We also have
\theta_{c^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime}}\cong(\underline{M}{}^{\prime}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L^{\prime}_{0}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}),V^{\prime})
, as we saw in the proof of Theorem
6.26
.

Now we need to express (the stable isomorphism class of)
\theta_{c^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime}}
in terms of
\theta_{U,F}
. We already know how
\theta_{c^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime}}
is related to
\theta_{U^{\prime},F}
, and by Theorem
6.19
we have
\theta_{U^{\prime},F}\mathrel{\underset{{}^{\scriptstyle{s}}}{\cong}}\theta_{U,F}
. However, we now need to know what happens to the
\underline{H}_{2}
component of
\theta_{U^{\prime},F}\cong(\underline{M}{}^{\prime}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};L^{\prime}_{0}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),V^{\prime})
under this stable isomorphism. So we will go through the proof of Theorem
6.19
again, this time choosing the homomorphism
J
(that is needed for Lemma
6.24
) more carefully.

The homomorphism
H_{q}(S)\cong(\bar{M}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2})/(\bar{L}_{0}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}))\rightarrow H_{q}(W)
is surjective, because
S
is a
q
-skeleton of
W
(and we used Lemma
6.6
for the isomorphism), and the image of (the coset of)
(0,(1,0))
is by definition
\alpha
, so there is an induced homomorphism
H_{q}(S)/\left<[0,(1,0)]\right>\cong\bar{M}_{0}/\bar{L}_{0}\rightarrow H_{q}(W)/\left<\alpha\right>
, which is also surjective.

The homomorphism
H_{q}(S^{\prime})\cong H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow H_{q}(W)
is surjective too, and the image of
(0,1)
is
\alpha
, so there is an induced homomorphism
H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(W)/\left<\alpha\right>
, which is also surjective.

By Lemma
2.21
a) there are integers
l,m\geq 0
and an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus\bar{M}_{0}/\bar{L}_{0}
which commutes with the maps to
H_{q}(W)/\left<\alpha\right>
(where the
\mathbb{Z}^{l}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
components are mapped to
0
). We fix such an isomorphism.

Consider the homomorphisms
\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\rightarrow H_{q}(W)
and
\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus\bar{M}_{0}/\bar{L}_{0}\rightarrow H_{q}(W)
(where the
\mathbb{Z}^{l}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
components are again mapped to
0
). After composing with the projection
H_{q}(W)\rightarrow H_{q}(W)/\left<\alpha\right>
, these two homomorphisms become equal, therefore their difference is a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\rightarrow\left<\alpha\right>
. Since
\left<\alpha\right>
is cyclic and
\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})
is free, this homomorphism factors as a composition
\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\left<\alpha\right>
, where the second homomorphism maps
1
to
\alpha
. By adding the first homomorphism of this composition to the previously fixed isomorphism, we get a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus\bar{M}_{0}/\bar{L}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus H_{q}(S)
which commutes with the maps to
H_{q}(W)
. We can extend it to an isomorphism
J:\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus H_{q}(S)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus\bar{M}_{0}/\bar{L}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
by sending
(0,0,1)
to
(0,0,1)
. This isomorphism
J
again commutes with the maps to
H_{q}(W)
.

Let
\tilde{c}
be an
m
-fold stabilisation of
c
and
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
be an
l
-fold stabilisation of
c^{\prime}
. Let
\tilde{S}
and
\tilde{S}^{\prime}
denote the
q
-skeletons of
W
with respect to
\tilde{c}
and
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
, then
H_{q}(\tilde{S})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus H_{q}(S)
and
H_{q}(\tilde{S}^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime})
. Moreover,
J:H_{q}(\tilde{S}^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{l}\oplus H_{q}(S^{\prime})\rightarrow H_{q}(\tilde{S})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus H_{q}(S)
is an isomorphism which commutes with the homomorphisms to
H_{q}(W)
. By applying Lemma
6.24
as in the proof of Lemma
6.23
, we get a homotopy equivalence
j:\tilde{S}^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{S}
that commutes with the inclusions in
W
(up to homotopy) such that
H_{q}(j)=J
.

Let
\tilde{U}
and
\tilde{U}^{\prime}
be regular neighbourhoods of
\tilde{S}
and
\tilde{S}^{\prime}
respectively. Then the composition
\tilde{S}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\rightarrow}}\tilde{S}\rightarrow\tilde{U}
is a homotopy equivalence and the composition
\tilde{S}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\rightarrow}}\tilde{S}\rightarrow\tilde{U}\rightarrow W
is homotopic to the inclusion of
\tilde{S}^{\prime}
. By Theorem
6.17
b) there is a diffeomorphism
g:\tilde{U}^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{U}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{\tilde{S}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\tilde{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tilde{U}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\tilde{U}}

commutes up to homotopy. This implies that
H_{q}(g)=J:H_{q}(\tilde{U}^{\prime})\cong H_{q}(\tilde{S}^{\prime})\rightarrow H_{q}(\tilde{U})\cong H_{q}(\tilde{S})
. Moreover,
g
is isotopic to the inclusion
\tilde{U}^{\prime}\rightarrow W
, and as we saw in the proof of Proposition
6.16
, such a
g
determines an isomorphism
I=H_{q}(g\big|_{\partial\tilde{U}^{\prime}}):\theta_{\tilde{U}^{\prime},F}\rightarrow\theta_{\tilde{U},F}
. Our next goal is to understand this isomorphism
I
.

Since
\tilde{c}
is an
m
-fold stabilisation of
c
, we have
\theta_{\tilde{U},F}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2m}\oplus\theta_{U,F}
by Lemma
6.22
. Let
\underline{\tilde{M}}_{0}=\underline{H}_{2m}\oplus\underline{\bar{M}}_{0}
,
\tilde{L}_{0}=(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{m})\oplus\bar{L}_{0}
and
\tilde{V}=(\mathbb{Z}^{m}\times\{0\})\oplus\bar{V}
, then
\theta_{\tilde{U},F}\cong(\underline{\tilde{M}}_{0}\oplus\underline{H}_{2};\tilde{L}_{0}\oplus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}),\tilde{V})
.

Similarly,
